A.21 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Provide an executive summary of the project/program/group:

1. Climate change problems: Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change and is ranked as the 9th most affected country to climate change according to the Climate Risk Index 2020. Terai region of Nepal is the most affected region in the country compared to Hills and Mountains in terms of death toll due to disasters. Terai is bordered by Indian Gangetic plain in the south and the Churia Physiographic Region in the north.

Penetration of clean cooking solution technologies is very low in Terai. According to the 2011 census, around 56% households used solid fuel (wood, dung cakes) for cooking, while 38% used liquefied petroleum gas (LPG), whereas during the same period, 56% household in Terai used fuel wood, 22% cow dung and 15% LPG. Burning of solid fuels releases large amounts of particulate matter, which is a major source of indoor air pollution, black carbon and GHGs (such as carbon dioxide, methane). Even though, fuel-wood consumption in Terai has remained fairly constant, LPG consumption is in increasing trend, as around 40% of the households used LPG for cooking. The country has been importing LPG since 2018/19 and has imported 357,600 MT of LPG from India during 2018/19 to 2019/20. Nepal imported and consumed 449,063 MT of LPG in 2019/20. LPG has to be imported, it has negative impacts on the economy due to widening trade deficit with India, and has made the country very susceptible to supply risk as well.

Nepal relies heavily on hydropower for its electricity generation, which is a clean and renewable energy source with no CO2 emissions. Currently it has installed capacity of around 1278 MW electricity of which 95% is from hydroelectricity. Nepal has enormous potential for hydro-electricity, touted around 63,000 MW (80%) of which is untapped. However, the Terai region has better water access and electricity access rate, compared to Hills and Mountains'. However, use of electricity for cooking has yet to pick up, as merely 0.08% of the target population relies on it.

2. Proposed Interventions

To mitigate the impacts of climate change and strengthen resilience of the most vulnerable communities to adapt to climate change, Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEP) has proposed to implement the project "Mitigating GHG emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean cooking solutions (CCS)". The project will be implemented in Nepal by AEP with financial support from the Green Climate Fund. It aims at mitigating climate change impacts on vulnerable communities of Nepal by: (i) Introducing innovative concepts in Nepal of (ii) bulk tendering via reverse auctioning for cost effectiveness; (iii) output based financing for de-risking of investments and (iv) mainstreaming and capacitating local governments to ensure long-term sustainability. The project will be implemented in 10 districts across the country up to now and could be game changers in the clean cooking sector. The proposed project will collaborate with Nepal Electricity Authority (NEA) to explore and develop cost effective tariff structure innovations for CCS, which will be piloted at the project locations. The project will also support NEA in the upgrading of electricity power supply in the project locations ensuring reliability, continuity and affordability. The project will be implemented through a public-private partnership model with participation in each stage of value chain, and especially create jobs and volunteering opportunities for them. For the first time in Nepalese cookstove sector, it aims to generate women technical workforce.

The project aims to promote clean cooking solutions, such as Tier 3+ Improved Cooking Stoves (ICS), Biogas and Electric Cook Stoves (ECS) in 10,000,000 Households (HHs) of Terai region. It will be implemented through a public-private partnership model with participation in each stage of value chain. The project targets to switch 500,000 households from LPG and wood-stove cookers to electric stoves; 490,000 households from LPG and wood-stove cookers to Tier 3+ ICS; and to introduce biogas system for 10,000 households in the project locations. Electric cooking is one of the potential clean cooking solutions for Nepal's 68.7% of electricity from hydropower.

### Climate impacts/benefits

The project will help to overcome significant barriers from transitioning traditional inefficient cooking practice to an efficient and climate friendly cooking solutions. Scaling up the government initiative on CCS, the proposed project aims at reducing an estimated 5.1 million tons of GHGs by bringing transformative change in the energy sector through Tier 3+ ICS, biogas and ECS. The project will also help to reduce deforestation and forest degradation due to reduced demand for fuel wood consumption. It is estimated that, Tier 3+ ICS can reduce fuel wood consumption by 30% on average and biogas and ECS will significantly reduce the use of fuel wood; in addition, more than 50% reduction in indoor air pollution is expected by replacing TCS by Tier 3+ ICS, which will primarily benefit women and children as women

#### Context: Describe the project location and intervention needs.
Briefly describe the target recipient of the proposed interventions including information on the demographics, economy, topography, etc. Nepal is a landlocked country bordered by India and China. Topographically, it is divided into three ecological zones: the Terai plains in the south, the Himalayan mountain range in the north, and the Indian Ganges plain in the south and the Churia Physiographic Region in the north. The project will be implemented in 22 districts of the Terai region, including some parts of the Churia/Figure 1). Terai is Nepal's most populous region with a population density of about 300 people per square kilometer and a population. It has very fertile soil and thus is known as the country's rice basket. It consists of dense forests- about 20.4% of its Terai area.

Access to clean energy cooking solutions is a massive problem for Nepal, including Terai. "Nepal depends heavily on traditional biomass energy sources, such as firewood, animal waste and crop residues for cooking purposes. According to the 2011 census, around 75% of the populations were using traditional biomas-63% used fuel wood, while 10.3% relied on cow dung (cake) stoves. This has not changed much in recent years. The Nepal Energy Sector Development Plan (ESDP) 2011 showed that still 73.5 % of the households depend on firewood for cooking. Furthermore, majority of them are using highly inefficient traditional cookstoves [TCS]. These TCS do not only consume more fuel, thus putting a strain on the environment and increasing deforestation but also lead to poor indoor air quality (efficiency of 10%), cause health problems and emit a massive amount of black carbon and GHGs (such as carbon dioxide, methane).

Household combustion is estimated to produce 25% of global emissions of black carbon, which is the second largest contributor to climate change after carbon dioxide (CO₂). World Health Organization (WHO) estimates 8,700 deaths a year from indoor air pollution in Nepal.

## Figure 1: Project Location

![](image_of_map.png)

In Terai, as cow dung are more easily available and free to collect, households rely heavily on dung cake as cooking fuel; it is used by 25% of the population. This cooking pattern is typical to the Terai region compared to other topography of the country as the dependence on dung cake in hills and mountainous regions is less due to the availability of firewood. Dung cakes are produced at the household level and burnt in traditional open fires or mud stoves, resulting in the emission of a dangerous cocktail of hundreds of pollutants to which women and young children are exposed daily.

While TCS is predominantly used for cooking in the rural areas, Liquefied Petroleum Gas (LPG) is the most commonly used cooking technology in urban areas due to its smoke-free operation and flexibility in use. In 2011 census, around 21% of the entire population were using it, while the 2019 World Bank survey showed that share had increased to 34.9%. LPG consumption is even higher in Terai region; it has increased from 58% to 76%.

Even though LPG is a cleaner fuel compared to wood and dung, as it does not produce smoke and contribute to indoor air quality, it is derived from fossil fuel, hence contributes to global GHG emissions. Moreover, LPG has to be imported from India; in 2018-19, Nepal imported 429,609 MT of LPG, which is a 27% increase compared to the previous year. This dependency on India for LPG supply poses a risk to Nepal's energy security situation, as it has not only widened the trade deficit but also made Nepal very susceptible to supply risks experienced during the 2015 fuel crisis when the supply of fossil fuels from Nepal to India was interrupted.

Electricity is free from load shedding and is increasingly being considered for cooking in rural areas. Nepal has enormous potential for hydroelectric generation, which is touted to be around 83,000 MW until couple of years back. Nepal was reeling under an acute power shortage that resulted in up to 16 hours of load shedding per day during the winter months. However, now the time is ideal to intervene with electric cooking, despite the benefit it offers. However, now the time is ideal to intervene with electric cooking, as the country is free from load shedding and by 2030, Government of Nepal has envisioned to increase electrification in rural areas. The government plans to connect all existing hydropower projects so that a hydropower project set to be connected to the grid within 2020. Currently, nearly 95.7% of the total electricity generation is supplied by hydro power projects. With new addition of hydro projects the share

of electricity would be cleaner. A simulation conducted by Nepal Electricity Authority, the state owned utility, shows that Nepal will be in the state of seasonal surplus by 2027 from which Nepal will be a pure exporter of power on RoY (Round-the-year) basis[5]. With an annual electrical consumption growth rate of 10%, Nepal cannot use surplus power because it lacks energy-intensive industries and electric mass-transit systems.

At present, 80% of populations of Nepal are connected to national grid. Terai has relatively high electricity access rate compared to Hills and Mountain[6]. However, use of electricity for cooking has yet to pick up, as most people of the region still rely on traditional biomass fuels such as firewood and dung. Only about half of the households have grid connection. For those who are not connected to grid would be offered the facility of Tier 3+ ECS and biogas. As a consequence, there will be no CO2 emissions as electricity from hydroelectric power plants is clean and renewable energy source. The government has also decided to reduce national electricity consumption. Electricity Regulation Commission of Nepal has made reforms to the household electricity tariff rates by reducing the cost structure by 10% so as to incentivize increase in use of electric appliances[7]. Nepal Electricity Authority has publicly requested to use the electric appliances in the kitchen as much as possible.

AEPc has conducted a user survey along 284 local governments among the 22 districts of Terai Region, spread across six Provinces, regarding the usage of cooking solutions[8]. The survey provides baseline data on the current status and future trends of cooking energy use in the Terai region. In Terai region, 58.13% use TCS, 3.26% use biogas plants, 40.24% use LPG, and 0.43% use electric induction stoves. Through this project, AEPc has envisioned replacing 500,000 TCS and Tier-1 ECS by 10,000 ECS and 60,000 biogas plants in the next five years[9]. The replacement of 324,000 LPG users to electric cookstoves (ECS). Transitioning from TCS and LPG to more cleaner cooking solutions would reduce unsustainable consumption of fuel wood, cow dung and LPG, and also create demand for electricity. However, the socio-economic conditions in the region are such that changes are less likely to occur on its own.TCS users are among the poorest and socially excluded groups.

Overall, HDI (Human Development Index) ranking for the Province, which encompasses most of the Terai region is very low[10]. Furthermore, the target beneficiaries that the project aims to intervene or the women, children and elderly people in the region have a high prevalence rate of poverty. The PPI reflects severe gender disparity (0.53 female to male ratio) in this region. Hence, affordability to switch to energy efficient technologies without external interventions remains a challenge.

In the absence of the project intervention, there would be no reduction in solid biomass and LPG consumption. This will further aggravate indoor air pollution and GHG emissions.

Related project/interventions: Also describe any recent or ongoing projects/interventions that are related to the proposal from other domestic or international sources of funding, such as the Global Environment Facility, Adaptation Fund, Climate Investment Funds, etc., and how they will be complemented by this proposal. With the launch of the global campaign of Clean Cooking Solution for all (CCSALL) initiated in 2013, the Government of Nepal had endorsed Investment Prospectus for clean cooking solutions [1] and Biomass Energy Strategy [2]. The BEST has the aim of attaining CCSSALL in two stages i.e. by improving solid biomass use by 2025 (i.e., 70% reduction of cooking with open fire burning) and modern solutions of cooking with at least Tier 3 level > 2030.

The Government of Nepal has announced the implementation of a multi-year programme: “Tiered Clean Cooking Programme”, starting from the fiscal year 2019/2020. Under this programme, traditional fuel such as animal dung and firewood will be replaced by Tier I, II, III, and IV clean cooking solutions for clean cooking. Despite many efforts by the Government of Nepal, a number of challenges and barriers continue to hinder the adoption of clean cooking technologies in rural areas especially in the mountain region. Government of Nepal and External Development Partners (EDPs) have invested around 4.5 million USD for CCS in fiscal year 2018/2019 [3]. Predominantly, AEPC distributed mud ICS which had immediate impact but the adoption rate was low due to lack of awareness and maintenance cost with a long lasting impact. Promotion of electric cooking is a shift from existing renewable energy technologies that are under the current subsidy regime. In terms of the implementation arrangement the technology will be promoted through public-private partnership (PPP) model in the mountain region which required massive investment and resources. Government and non-government agencies are implementing climate change initiatives in different parts of Nepal, but most of these programmes work with local communities without considering their long-term benefits by addressing their local needs. The Nepal Small Grants Programme primarily contributes to Goal 7: Ensure Access to Affordable Clean Energy. It also contributes to Goal 13: Take Urgent Action to Combat Climate Change and its Impacts; and Goal 15: Protect, Restore and Promote Sustainable Use of Terrestrial Ecosystems, Sustainably Manage Forests, Combat Desertification and Halt and Reverse Land Degradation.

The GEF Small Grants Programme (GSGP) is a global initiative that supports small-scale projects in developing countries. The GEF SGP has supported the installation of bio-gas, solar home and micro hydro systems, as well as solar toilets, rice cookers and water filters. The GSGP has reduced 120 tonnes of carbon dioxide due to these efforts. Furthermore, the Ministry of Forests and Environment (MoFE) with the Nepal Climate Change Support Program (NCCSP), with funding support from the UK Department for International Development (DFID), has been implementing a project on Community-based Flood Risk Management in Nepal. This project is under implementation with particular focus on the preparation and implementation of LAPAs. There are other projects such as the Community-based Flood Risk and GLOF Risk

Reduction Program, which has received funding from the Least Developed Countries (LDCs) Fund through the United Nations Development Program (UNDP), as the Global Environment Facility (GEF) implementing agency.

The proposed project is designed incorporating the lesson learnt from the past. The proposed project will create a market in itself; thus providing the initial big boost to the CCS market. In terms of technology deployment, the project will be implemented by adopting innovative approaches such as bulk tendering via reverse auctions, output based financing and capacity building for the private sector. By demonstrating the benefits of Tier 3+ ICS and Electric Cooking Thus, the project will catalyze the sector and underpin the scale up in future post the project intervention.

The incremental impact in the Gulf focuses exclusively on distribution of technology. During the FY 2018/19 less than 2% of the entire budget spent on programs under CCS was spent on activities in line with capacity development and strengthening of supply chain. The consequences of that gap can be seen at sales capacity at the user level which ultimately affects in up-scaling and dissemination of CCS.

In a business-as-usual (BAU) approach for deployment of CCS technologies, AEPIC sets annual target for technology deployment; fixes certain amount of subsidy for each technology for households and institutions. The number of subsidized units is determined by the total budget allocated to CCS. This is shortlisted based on a set of qualification criteria to implement those targets such shortlisted companies are called prequalified companies (PQ companies). PQ companies collect demand from households, and once the demand is collected they submit the demand to AEPIC. The demand is then collected by households and submit the installation completion form to AEPIC at which point they qualify to claim the annual subsidy amount from AEPIC. Finally, AEPIC conducts sample based inspection of units fitted in final year of the project.

The bureaucratic and lengthy process of this BAU approach has made it difficult for AEPIC to achieve its annual targets. The number of subsidized units is determined by the total budget allocated; thus if annual targets are predetermined, there is no incentive for PQ companies to reduce the cost of technology or innovate better technology. To shift towards clean cooking, Nepal requires a multi-pronged approach. First, "increase awareness and acceptance" among households about CCS technologies through campaigns 'do risk the investment by introducing the output based financing'. Second, ensure quality assurance and adequate after sales service by strengthening the existing testing centers and expanding its services to other regions. Third, provide subsidies for CCS technologies as it plays a crucial role in improving access to clean cooking solutions. Hence, their capacity needs to be developed.

Below mentioned point summarizes these barriers, as well as how the project will address them.


1. Socio-economic barriers

The main socio-economic barriers for transition to CCS include consumer's limited awareness about benefits of CCS and subsidy policy on renewable energy and their low willingness to pay higher prices due to economic levels of the households. Similarly, EPC users are unaware about the health benefits of Tier 3+ ICS and cost savings from using ECS. Likewise, ECS users are unaware about the health benefits of Tier 3+ ICS and

biogas. There is an existing subsidy provision for building domestic biogas, however many households are unaware of the subsidy scheme. In this context, users need more awareness raising and access to affordable financing to adopt CCS. Furthermore, household cooking is challenged from gender stereotyping and lack of knowledge.

The project will address the issue by bringing behavioral change among users by raising awareness about the cost and health benefits of ICS through campaigns and promotional activities. Also, the project will provide training on the use of ICS technologies in order to increase the adoption rate which it will reach the number of 1 million households in the Terai region of Nepal, the five year period that focuses on the promotion of CCS technologies.

The project will also address the issue by providing technical assistance and capacity building among the consumers of the Terai Region. The will ensure the acceptance of the proposed technology. Post project, the users will be able to use ICS technologies in their homes and communities given the change in behavior coupled with the intervention on the value chain through awareness and promotional campaigns, promotion of emerging CCS technologies, training and support for the establishment of a network of service providers and maintenance technicians. This will set a virtuous cycle in the sector through which further interventions will no longer be required and the uptake of the technologies will follow a natural course given the benefits and the change in behavior.

## 2. Technological barriers

Low level of technical knowledge and unavailability of technicians is another major constraint to shift towards CCS. Very few stove designs are available in the country and consumers face a lack of choice in terms of design, size, shape, material, color, etc., which makes it difficult for them to meet their user's requirements also is a challenge. Currently there are not so many manufacturers and vendors of Tier 3+ ICS and ECS in Nepal. They are being imported mainly from India and China. There is no technical support available locally as well. The lack of skilled technicians, especially in the remote areas with limited workforce availability; the scene is further alarming on women's involvement. At community level, capacity building, repair and maintenance training would eliminate such barrier.

The project will support capacity enhancement at community level, human resources/technical expertise development and strengthening of existing testing center. In addition, the project will strengthen the existing testing center to ensure the quality of the stoves. As stated in GAP, the project will address gender issues on technological aspects.

Access to grid electricity, quality of electricity power supply and voltage fluctuations are other challenges to shift towards electric cooking. The quality of electricity power supply in different parts of the country is not consistent as it is heavily dependent upon the weather conditions. Electric cooking would be promoted in the grid-connected households exploring and introducing a cost effective tariff structure for household consumption. In addition, the project will provide technical assistance on the installation of AEPC boards. In the short term, beneficiary selection will be a key process while disseminating the different types of technologies provisioned by the project. Electric Cooking will be mainly targeted based on the demand in the urban and peri-urban areas where there is access to grid electricity and also in the rural areas which have reliable electric supply. This will include selected clusters in rural settings, urban and peri-urban areas which will have the required infrastructure. As a corrective measure for short term, AEPC will be provided to households with no access to grid electricity but having their own solar panels, batteries and conductors in the overloaded areas. Thus beneficiary selection process is the specific measures to ensure the electric supply is reliable at the downstream household level. In terms of the medium and

## Financial Barriers

Most of the TCS users are among the poorest households, and they lack capital to switch to CCS technology. Furthermore, they are usually deprived of formal banking channel; hence do not have access to bank financing. Although few cooperatives provide loans for CCS at community level, the interest rate is very high (15-20 percent per annum). Therefore, a more affordable and effective tariff structure needs to be introduced, which is also an ongoing policy of the Ministry of Energy, Water Supply and Irrigation. As such, Electricity Regulatory Commission has revised the tariff structure for electric cookers in the rural areas from 30 percent discount on consumption up to 15 per unit up to 10 units per month, 25 percent discount for those consuming up to 150 units per month and 15 percent discount for those consuming more than 150 units. The revised tariff structure will be effective from January Year 2020/21. The revision has been made to promote Electric Cooking in the households. A further cost effective tariff structure will be introduced in coordination with Nepal Electricity Authority holding the increasing trend of revenue and in addition of earning opportunity to the cooperatives during project implementation.

In addition, there is a high investment risk due to sustainability financing mechanisms. Current subsidy-based approach is very expensive and lengthening projects that makes it difficult for the government to achieve big target in a short period. Buta tendency via reverse auctioning would help to reduce the cost of CCS technology. The World Bank’s Global Environment Facility (GEF), Green Climate Fund (GCF), GoF and other possible development partners would help in addressing the financial barriers also. Also, the procurement will follow bid tendering via reverse auctioning (least cost basis) approach for both equipment competitiveness and output based payment to de-risk the investment (detail has been included in section B3).

From the beneficiaries’ perspective, the targeted beneficiaries who shall be the poor households of the urban and peri-urban areas for electric cookstoves and poor households of the rural area for the Tier 3+ CCS lack sufficient income. The subsidy will be provided to cover a portion of their annual income up to the amount that needs to be invested on the installation (CAPEX) and operation (OPEX), considering a conservative approach of with bare minimum cost allocation household per year. The beneficiaries will have to pay back the subsidy from the project within 5% to 32% of their annual income on the CAPEX over the five-year period, and between 0% to 49% of their annual income on the OPEX over the five-year period. The percentage of income share level analysis has been presented as a separate annex and also in section D.6 of the funding proposal.

## Institutional Barriers

After the promulgation of new constitution in 2015, Nepal has become a federal republic with country being divided into seven Provinces. The old village development committees have been merged to


---
**Page 18**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 108 OF 113

create rural municipalities, while new municipalities and metropolitan cities have also been created. In a nutshell, Nepal has now different governance modality, with newly formed Local Governments (LG) and Province Government (PG). The new constitution has given mandate to the Local Governments for development planning and implementation. However, they have weak institutional setup vis-à-vis renewable energy technologies. They also lack:
* Technical and financial capacity.
* Awareness of renewable energy.
* Dedicated human resources.
* Expertise required for planning (energy plans), procuring (demand aggregation and bulk tendering approach), implementing and monitoring renewable energy technologies projects and programme.

The proposed project would empower LGs by heavily engaging them throughout the project implementation phase. The project will capitalize LGs and PGs to prioritize CCS promotion in their periodic and annual planning, formulating appropriate policies and mechanisms.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 17 OF 113

## Financial, ecological, institutional, etc., as relevant) that need to be addressed. Use a results chain of inputs, activities, outputs, outcomes, and impact statements, and identify the how and why of causal relations to deliver the project's expected results.

The primary problem that this project will address is increasing greenhouse emissions and indoor air pollution due to inefficient cooking practices. This project aims to mitigate the problem by increasing access to efficient cooking technologies (ECS) in Nepal towards a low carbon development path with increased climate resilience and sustainable development.

While high dependence on biomass fuel using inefficient ECS and growing use of LPG is in increasing trend, the transition to CCS is challenging. The set of barriers and risks that might prevent the project objectives from being achieved are as follows:

### B1. Inadequate capital and technical and operational cost: Most of the TCS users do not have access to tangible assets or borrow money on the capital market, banks and financial institutions lack loans products for CCS technologies. Similarly, the CAPEX of Tier 3+ ECS and biogas will be higher compared to ECS technology being replaced by TCS. In addition, the cost of LPG is increasing due to increased ECS usage in Nepal. The additional cost presented by presenting over may prevent a user to adopt new CCS technology. The selection criteria for the given technologies and the subsidized rate for each of the technologies are not clear enough to the users and the government and in the long run the project will help maneuver through the barrier by creating an enhanced awareness on the use and benefits of biogas technology ensuring long lasting usage of the proposed technologies.

### B2. Technical and operational barrier pertaining to adoption of new ECS: The abhorrence towards the ECS due to the smell, hygiene issues, and the risk to the natural grid, the quality of electricity on supply end is still weak in most areas of the country, which is a major barrier to self-propagate electric cooking in Nepal. As such, the currently available distribution system may not have the capacity to support additional load demand presented by ECS. Additionally, at household level, the electricity system will need to be upgraded for installing ECS; this will require additional time and capital on the user's part.

### B3. Lack of technical and project implementation skill at sub-national institutions: Most sub-national levels do not possess the technical capacity to emission and incorporating energy efficiency programmes in the annual energy plans. Likewise, the capacity may not be up to par for implementing projects of this scale.

### B4. Lack of awareness and limited access to CCS technology: There are not enough information, marketing and promotion of Tier 3+ ECS and Electric Cook Stoves readily available for general public. Most of the ECS users in Nepal are unaware about the benefits of ECS. Moreover, most of the LPG users are unaware of cost savings from Electric Cook Stoves. There is an existing subsidy for building domestic biogas; however many households are unaware of the subsidy scheme.

### B5. Lackking functional market approach for CCS: Because of their relatively recent introduction to the domestic market in Nepal, ECS have not yet been widely adopted by Tier 3+ ECS and Electric Cook Stoves in Nepal. Likewise, repair and maintenance centers, with limited skilled human


---
**Page 20**
---

resources are sparse, ever more so in the context of rural areas where a large portion of Tier +3 CCS will be deployed. This may present a risk to consumers for adoption of CCS due to limitation of after sales services.

B6. Gender disparity in CCS value chain, and inadequate opportunities and limited accessibility for women

Barriers to an equal representation up and down in value chain includes traditional social norms based against women, poverty as a result of limited access to education, early marriage, limited asset ownership. Furthermore, women's participation is limited to decision making and income generating activities, and technical work.

R1. Investment risk due to existing subsidy delivery mechanism: Current subsidy delivery mechanism is very bureaucratic and lengthy process that makes it difficult for users to access it, hence, for the government to achieve big target in a short period.

The project activities are based on three major targets:

Increasing access to CCS by deployment of proposed technologies

1. Deployment of CCS for scaling up the installation targets: This activity involves procurement and deployment of CCS technology at scale with a focus to those which will directly increase access to CCS. Likewise, the activity will use revised procurement and payment delivery methods in bulk tendering via reverse auctioning and output based payment that will ensure reduction in cost and risk associated with project implementation. The activity will address B2 and B1.

Strengthening quality assurance and enhancing product standards

2. Enhancing product standards: conducting assessments, surveys and analysis: Within this activity national level technical standards and documents will be prepared for CCS technologies proposed to be deployed under the project. Likewise, a comprehensive assessment of barriers pertaining to adoption of proposed CCS technology. Likewise, Project Implementation Unit (PIU) will also systematize procurement and subsidy delivery mechanisms as per project requirement. The result will address B2 and R2.

3. Strengthening quality assurance mechanisms: The activity will mainly focus on strengthening testing facilities and developing quality assurance mechanisms for procurement of CCS technologies. The activity will address B2.

Facilitating sub-national levels, enabling functional CCS market and increasing awareness of CCS at user level

4. Capacity development of sub-national institutions: Through this activity, subnational institutions will be capacitated to prioritize CCS promotion in their annual plans, formulating appropriate policies and mechanisms to achieve those targets. Likewise, the project will also assist local governments in preparing their energy master plans. The activity will address B3.


---
**Page 21**
---

5. Increasing awareness and outreach to enhance demand: Multiple awareness campaigns and promotional activities regarding CCS and its benefits will be conducted targeted towards beneficiaries. This activity will address B4.

6. Strengthening service centres, biomass manufacturers to provide quality and affordable clean cooking solutions: The activity will majorly focus on strengthening CCS supply chain as well as the capacity of local technology manufacturers, entrepreneurs and beneficiaries. The activity will address B5.

The project results that would be achieved by conducting the aforementioned activities are as follows:

* Increase in proportion of households adopting CCS technology: This indicates the total increase in number of households who have adopted CCS technology.
* Increase affordability of CCS technology through bulk tendering via reverse auctioning approach: Bulk tendering via reverse auctioning reduces the initial price of CCS in comparison to R&D based pricing.
* Quality assurance mechanisms for CCS is operationalized: National level standards on CCS will be specified; likewise, one central level testing facilities for CCS technologies will be strengthened.
* Household fully adapted CCS technologies with changed behavior: The project targets 90% of the total households will continue the use of CCS technology.
* A supply chain of trained and skilled personnel to ensure functional local CCS service centres will be supported for maintenance through dedicated vocational trainings to individuals.
* Sub-centres will be established in each district to provide services to the community. Each LG will be assigned a dedicated CCS facilitator and will be supported in creating dedicated energy units. Likewise, the project will support LGs in preparing energy master plans and assist in developing local action plans for CCS technology.
* Enhanced environment for women's engagement at top down value chain: Institutional and managerial structures will be established to ensure gender equality in planning, implementation & evaluation. Extensive use of GESI specialist during the project implementation period and gender sensitization training for project staff at all levels to maintain desired level of gender awareness. The project will also ensure that a dedicated gender focal person in institutional setup will assure increased employment and volunteer opportunities to women.

These envisioned project results will help us in achieving following project level outcomes:

5.0 Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for low-emission planning and development (Results linked to the outcome: 4, 6, and 7)
7.0 Lower energy intensity of buildings, cities, industries and appliances (Results linked to the outcome: 1, 2, & 3)

The ultimate final level impact that this project will achieve is:

* Reduced emission from buildings, cities, industries and appliances

## B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

The project aims to use Clean Cooking Solutions by instigating the concepts of (i) bulk tendering via reverse auctioning (least cost based selection for cost effectiveness), (ii) Output based financing for de-risking investments and (iii) mainstreaming and capacitating local governments in the renewable energy sector. The project will focus on three core components as listed below:

1. **Bulk Tendering via Reverse Auctioning Enhances CCS Quality, Reduces Costs and Increases Access to Appropriate CCS Technologies**
2. **Local Government Leads Development of Municipal Energy Plan (MEP) and Promotes CCS with Identifying Demand Linked with Online Management Information System**
3. **Output Based Financing Mechanism Ensures Sustainable CCS Services and De-Risks Investment in the Renewable Energy Sector**

# COMPONENT 1: SCALING UP THE DEPLOYMENT OF CLEAN COOKING TECHNOLOGIES THROUGH ACCELERATED INVESTMENT AND MARKET DEVELOPMENT

## Output 1.1: 500,000 Electric Stoves, 490,000 Tier 3+ ICS and 10,000 biogas plants installed.

Under component 1, the proposed project will support growth of clean cooking solution in Tera.

### Activity 1.1.1: Development of Annual Procurement and Deployment plan

Based on the above-mentioned output, a cluster wise deployment procedure will be prepared at regional or provincial level. Different bidding documents for all these CCS technologies will be prepared to procure the technologies through bulk tendering via public auction/ reverse auction as per the Public Procurement Act, 2007 (PPA) and Public Procurement Regulation, 2007 (PPR). The reverse auctioning model refers to the Least Cost Selection (LCS) Method of the PPR. LCS method ensures that technology is procured at least cost. The procurement will follow a reverse auction/LCS mechanism as per PPAPPR.

| Step 1: Preparation of Bid Documents |
| --- |
| Step 2: Notice Publication |
| Step 3: Pre-Bid Meeting |
| Step 4: Bid Submission |
| Step 5: Evaluation |
| Step 6: Letter of Intent |
| Step 7: Letter of Acceptance and Agreement |

### Figure: Least Cost Selection Process (LCS)

#### Activity 1.1.2: Procurement and Deployment of annual targeted number of CCS

The project aims to deploy 490,000 Tier 3+ ICS, 10,000 Domestic Biogas Plants and 500,000 ECS over a five-year period. The beneficiary and technology will be selected by a two-step process: 1) eligibility criteria and 2) prioritization.

Eligibility criteria includes mandatory technical feasibility such as:
- Gas supply
- Connection to the grid
- i.15 Amp meter connection for ECS,


---
**Page 24**
---

### Fuel accessibility and affordability for ICS

**Prioritization will be made based on socio-economic vulnerability indicators scoring system (such as level of income disability in the family, female headed households, single women, widows, disabled people, and affordability factor). Further in terms of the beneficiary selection in case of the respective technologies, the following ecological and economic factors shall be taken into consideration. The electricity access is considered to be a pre-requisite for ICS adoption. The average income range between $250 to $3000. The income distribution has been further been presented in section D.4.**

Similarly, project location and demand from LD will be subsidiary factors in beneficiary selection. Vendor selection will be based on Standard Public Procurement Process. Prequalified companies identified by AEP for specific technologies will be the potential vendors. Least Cost based selection method as per Public Procurement Acts and Regulation will be adapted.

Furthermore, a detailed implementation plan is required in detail during the project implementation stage while preparing the standard operating procedure/operational manual for the project.

Following are the target beneficiaries of ICS technology with the minimum annual consumption required:

* **0.5 Million ECS** are targeted for households using LPG and fuel wood with reliable electricity access.
* **6.48 Million Tier 3+ ICS** are targeted for households using loose biomass, dung cake and fuel wood with or no/ bad quality electricity access.
* **Finally, 0.01 million biogas systems** are targeted for households having sufficient cattle/westocks and livestock waste.

Switching from the TCB to Biogas plant for a typical family requires an upfront capital, the subsidized rate, therefore lowers the burden on the household. Further, the longer lifespan of biogas plants adds tremendous value. The annual operational cost of pigies for the households with cattle and live stocks is significantly reduced as compared to other energy sources. This will also reduce the environmental impact associated with fuel consumption for bio-gas technology as the targeted beneficiaries of biogas technology are envisioned to have cattle at home, therefore providing a free source of animal and agricultural waste that can be used in the production of biogas. The use of biogas technology increases the efficiency factor when understood by the beneficiaries which will trigger a widespread utilization and replication of the technology.

Similarly, in case TCB and ICS, ICS consumes more fuel (2500-3000 kg per annum) than ICS which consumes 1500 kg per annum). We have envisioned a higher scale of subsidized rate compared to biogas and electric cook stoves, considering that the targeted beneficiaries will be on the lower strata of financial and economic capabilities. A widespread utilization and replication of the technology will be triggered when beneficiaries understand the fuel wood consumption ratio, health benefits and efficiency factor.

Domestic biogas is developed on site locally whereas electric cookstoves, Tier 3+ ICS, and accessories of domestic biogas are imported. Pre-tax subsidies are available to import ICS/ Tier 3+ raw materials for ICS and Biogas systems at a subsidized rate of approximately 1%. In addition, GCF has reduced custom charges in induction cookstoves from 15% to 5%, promoting the use of ECS.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 23 OF 113

Out of the total 294 LGs in the 22 districts, the project targets 150 LGs selected based on demand of CCS. There are about 1.5 million households in these 150 LGs. While selecting the beneficiary households, priority will be given to economically impoverished and socially excluded families, indigenous people, single women, female-headed households and widows.

The annual deployment target is given below:

| Technologies | Year 1 | Year 2 | Year 3 | Year 4 | Year 5 | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Electric Cook Stoves | 50,000 | 100,000 | 135,000 | 135,000 | 80,000 | 500,000 |
| Tier 3+ ICS | 50,000 | 100,000 | 130,000 | 130,000 | 80,000 | 490,000 |
| Domestic Biogas Plant | 2,000 | 2,000 | 2,000 | 2,000 | 2,000 | 10,000 |

In addition to the annual deployment target, the unit cost and the funding proportions of each of the financiers is given below:

| SN | Technology | Unit Cost (USD) | GCF | AEPIC | LGs | Beneficiaries |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Tier 3+ ICS | $ 48.70 | - | - | - | 100% |
| 2 | Biogas | $ 677.08 | - | - | - | 100% |
| 3 | ECS | $ 47.92 | - | - | - | 100% |

The Procurement of CCS technologies for this project will follow the following process:

Bulk Tendering via Reverse Auctioning (Least Cost Based Selection Method as per the Public Procurement Act/Public Procurement Regulations of Nepal). The bulk tendering process will be inclusive and follow bottom up approach and follow the PPA/PPR. The LG level staffs will collect demand from households in their respective LGs and submit these demands to the District Management Information System (MIS). MIS will automate the demand aggregation process. This will provide a list of all CCS technologies that are required by the LGs. Based on this list, a cluster of CCS deployment; these demands would be pooled into different clusters, each cluster encompassing different LGs based on geographical convenience. The entire one million CCS deployment target would be translated to 150 LGs and then further divided among the LGs as per their individual CCS deployment targets. The project targets have been determined to align with the country priorities. For instance, the Second NDC Nepal has priorities on the clean cooking sector that will be to ensure 15% of the total households in the country are using clean cooking technologies by 2030.

<sup>1</sup> Detail on MIS is mentioned in Activity 2.2.2

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 34 OF 113

ensure 25% of households use electric stoves by 2025, install 500,000 improved cook stoves specifically in rural areas and by 2025, install an additional 200,000 household biogas plants.

In the event of variances, an informed guess can be made stating the overall demand from the LGs will be on the higher side. Here the beneficial selection criteria will assess the demands and LGs can offer first come first serve basis. The procurement strategy for the above mentioned technologies will be developed as the Standard Operating Procedure is prepared during the project implementation stage.

APEC will then call for tender via reverse auction as per PPAPRPR to procure the technologies, for each cluster. As there are three different technologies involved, separate tenders would be called for each of the technologies. Based on the demand received, clusters will be formed between LGs in terms of geographical convergence. The exact formation of these clusters will be decided during the implementation stage based on the demand received and the geographical convergence.

The quantity for an optimum reverse auctioning price point will be assessed during the procurement. The procurement strategy will be prepared by APEC and submitted to GCF for approval. The tender documents will be issued by APEC with the public procurement rules and regulations to prepare the tender documents. Lowest bidders that comply with all the norms would be awarded the contracts.

The reverse auction approach is expected to bring down the cost of technology significantly, through competition and the economies of scale. It is estimated that the cost of the technologies will further go down by 20% after the reverse auctioning process. Furthermore, the requirement of continuous testing in choosing the requirement of technology before the auction, ensures quicker adoption and maximum utilization of the deployed CCS technologies.

| SN | Technology | Unit Cost (USD) | GCF | APEC | LGs | Beneficiaries |

(period) The 20% will be financed by the GCF and AE. A batch verification process will be triggered at this stage. The batch will be tested at the Renewable Energy Testing Station (RETS). ## Activity 1.1: Monitoring and Verification of Installed CCS units for Output Based Financing (OBF)

Output based financing is another breakthrough approach compared to the BAU process, wherein subsidies are being provided based on installation of the technologies. In this BAU scenario, the vendor was collecting demand and completing installations at households across the country. Post installation, the vendor was then claiming the applicable subsidy amount from AEPC. At this stage, the vendor was released 100% upon satisfaction of the quality assurance. This approach had several risks for both the vendors and AEPC as it minimized its risk by demanding upfront capital from the vendors. However, it has failed to address the risk of non-utilization of the technology by the households after the installation. This BAU model also had another major drawback: As vendors were required to put upfront capital, they were bearing more risks. As such, they were reluctant to collect demand and complete installations at households before their installation targets.

The proposed OBF approach would provide upfront capital for vendors using them leverage to service more households. With full disbursement tied to the independent third party monitoring, major chunk of the payment will be released only after the verification of the technologies, which guarantees a quality assurance. This would create a win-win situation for both the vendors and AEPC.

The output based financing approach will have several mechanisms set in place. Firstly, the technical specification of the required technologies will be thoroughly tested. Mobilization advance amount not exceeding 60% will be provided to the vendor upon clearance from the procurement. The technologies will be taken to the respective municipalities. And upon clearance better than 95%, the remaining disbursement will be provided to the vendors. At this stage, total disbursement not exceeding 60% will be provided to the vendor.

The vendors, then in coordination with LGs, will install CCS technologies at the identified households. Simultaneously, a separate third party monitoring process will be conducted parallel to distribution of the technologies wherein the third party will specifically assess appropriate percentage of total beneficiaries in terms of (i) reduction in carbon emissions through replacement of coal fuel with LPG, (ii) reduction in consumption of firewood and other biomass fuels through replacement of fuel wood, dung cake and LPG, and (iii) adaptation benefits in terms of vulnerable communities. The third party will be selected by the GCF and AEPC on a competitive basis based on sub-categories such as gender income status and so on. The third party will be selected via procurement following the IPPRA. Request for proposal will be floated to all qualified firms. Once the third party is selected, 40% of the total disbursement will be provided to the vendor. Hence, at this stage, the vendor will be paid 100%


---
**Page 28**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 28 OF 113

of the total amount, AEPIC's experience with various renewable energy technologies including the solar home system, biogas and cookstoves that a substantial change is required in the existing payment approach. The 90-10% approach reduces the ownership of the vendors and does not bolster the quality assurance mechanism. This will be addressed by developing a robust quality assurance mechanism for post installation or dissemination. The output based financing approach will shift the paradigm from the existing 90–10 % payment modality and create ownership of the vendors and engage them throughout the process.

In terms of the beneficiary contribution to this project, this will be under the discretion of the LGs. The beneficiaries will either contribute during the installation or during the demand collection. The LGs can also opt for partial or full payments during the various stages. A detailed operational modality will be developed during the implementation phase.

A pilot study conducted by GIZ/NEED on a “loan based financing project” clearly indicates the need of designated capacity building activities for successful implementation of CCS project. To address this issue, the project has designed adequate number of capacity building activities. Another scope for improvement is to provide 60% advance payment to the vendors. The existing financial institutions have been limiting their capacity to invest more. Hence, the project will provide up to 60% of advance payment to motivate the vendors.

Activity 2.1.2: Update existing standards^30 and set benchmarks for the technologies required for the project implementation.

The existing standards on CCS will be analysed and benchmarks will be set for the technologies required for project implementation. As standards on induction stoves^31, ICS, and domestic biogas plants have been set previously, these standards will be reviewed and updated.


AEPIC have developed an interim benchmark for solid biomass burning cook stoves "Nepal Interim Benchmark for Solid Biomass Cook Stoves (NBBCS, 2016)", in a close coordination with the Nepal Alliance for Clean Cooking (NACC), National Accreditation Board (NAB), National Environment Service (NESM) and other relevant stakeholders. It categorizes the solid biomass stoves in three different types i.e. chimmey stove, chemical stove and multi-tier stove. The NBBCS also defines the performance of these stoves based on their fuel efficiency, total emissions, indoor emissions, safety score, and durability. As part of the project activities, this benchmark will be updated through regular interaction and consultations meetings with national stove testing laboratories, NACC, NESM, and feedback from international product updates on stove testing.


For ECS, the standard will be developed to cover different types of stoves, including induction, infrared, coal, etc. The performance metrics for ECS will include fuel efficiency, emissions, indoor air quality, and other performance metrics. In collaboration with Nepal Electricity Authority (NEA), a further cost effective tariff structure for electric cooking will be introduced analyzing the increment in the revenue due to increased electricity consumption. A detailed plan of action on how the government would support and facilitate would be done to ensure quality of electric power supply in the tariff referring the performance report of the stoves which is generated from the Management Information System (MIS). Gender inclusive and diversity friendly boards will be formed in the formulation or upgrading of above mentioned normative and standardized tools.


It is to be noted that the NEA will not be involved in any of the financial, procurement, financial arrangement, monitoring and evaluation including project completion. Its role will be only in the areas of strengthening the tariff structure for electric cooking and improving electricity supply in the areas where electric cookstoves will be deployed. The collaboration in fact will involve coordination between the AECC and NEA. The performance report generated from the MIS will be used to ensure that the tariffs have been determined to align with the country priorities. Nepal's SDG 7 targets to achieve the electrification rate of 90 percent and the Second NDC aims to strengthen transmission and distribution links to support up scaling of electric cooking in the country. The AECC has prioritized on the clean cooking sector which is to ensure 15% of the total energy demand is supplied from renewable energy sources. In Residential cooking by 2030, ensure 25% of households


---
**Page 30**
---

use electric stoves by 2025, install 500,000 improved cook stoves, specifically in rural areas and by 2025, install an additional 200,000 household biogas plants. In terms of electric cooking and strengthening transmission, AEPC and NEA being public entities are responsible to the Government of Nepal to ensure that both entities fulfill their respective responsibilities to achieve the targets. In 2015, when there was a shortage of cooking gas in the market, many depended on electricity to meet their demand for cooking resulting in an unexpected rise in demand. The project will address this issue by ensuring that households are connected with regular mapping of distribution systems based on their capacity and load status in order avoid such situation where the supply is not sufficient. Areas which have a high number of connections but are often under loaded, are potential start points for the project. This is where the project activities conducted by AEPC will support the NEA. The AEPC will approach NEA in terms of providing information on upgrading and strengthening transmission lines to ensure connection lines to upgrade and control voltage fluctuations in the project area. This will ensure that NEA is well-informed on the existing capacity and streamline their upgradation at the identified project location.

### Activity 2.1: Develop Municipal Energy Plan template by analyzing standards and formats

The project will develop a Municipal Energy Plan template by analyzing existing standards and formats.
The MEP design takes participatory model with sufficient stakeholder consultation, which primarily includes representatives from pum indigenous community, women led enterprises and organizations, key LG officials, service-supply and service-demand stakeholders.

The household situation in terms of adequate infrastructure for ensuring reliable and prolonged application of electric cookstoves will be a key factor taken into consideration. In addition to the household capacity, the project proponent will also consider the availability of grid capacity up to 15 amperes where electric cooking is feasible at all households. Furthermore, the additional demand will require enhancement of the existing transformers in the project location. Here, the project proponent will again collaborate with NEA and AEPC for ensuring that the necessary arrangements and infrastructure are in place. This is critical component of the project which will dismiss the rebound effect to LPG.

Activity 3.1.1: Conduct general inclusive national level stakeholder consultation workshop with AEPC, PG and LGs

Activity 3.1.2: Develop LG Municipal Energy Plan

Municipal Energy Plans would be developed to identify best available resources at local level for energy generation and distribution to meet the needs of households. The MEP design takes participatory model with significant stakeholder consultation, which primarily includes representatives from Indigenous community, women led enterprises and organizations, key LG officials, service-supply and service-demand sectors, and other relevant stakeholders. This will ensure that all voices are heard in selecting appropriate technology options to implement clean cooking initiatives. This will create an environment wherein MEFs will become a critical element of the annual budgeting and planning process and hence, will be supported by the LG governments. While preparing MEPs, complementarities will be ensured with ongoing other programmes/projects.

The process to prepare MEP include following nine steps:

Step 1: Identify Stakeholders to ensure a broad level of engagement from a cross-sector of energy players

Step 2: Establish an Energy Sub-Committee Team to lead development of energy plan, and to implement the plan and monitor progress. Step 3: Develop Energy Baseline to provide a holistic and updated picture on current energy situation in terms of energy access, energy poverty and clean cooking

Step 4: Develop Energy Vision, Mission and Goal to reflect overarching commitment and purpose.

Step 5: Develop Activities in collaboration with the Project, LGs with its regional offices and other stakeholders to ensure that activities are achievable, sustainable, mission aligned.

Step 6: Evaluate and Prioritize Activities to remain focused, resource-efficient and address immediate energy needs.

Step 7: Identify a Data Source to ensure smooth implementation of prioritized activities.

Step 8: Compile and Finalize the Plan to compile all information from all planning stages into a single plan document that is easy to understand and implement.

Step 9: Implementation and Monitoring & Evaluation to implement a plan assesses the progress and formulates necessary modifications to ensure maximum impact.

## Provide information on governance arrangements (supervisory boards, consultative groups among others) set to oversee and guide project implementation. Provide a composition of the decision-making body and oversight functions, particularly for Enhanced Direct Access (EDA) projects.

The Ministry of Finance will engage with a wide range of stakeholders, ensuring full stakeholder engagement from project inception through to monitoring and evaluation (M&E). AEPC will implement the project in close collaboration with a wide range of stakeholders, including Local Governments, private sector entities, civil society organizations, women groups, single women, and administrative supervisors of AEPC. Following the Government of Nepal's rules and procedures regarding the acquisition of project services, supplies, and works. The project work plan and budget will be approved by the Ministry of Finance after consultation with stakeholders.

The project will engage with a wide range of stakeholders in accordance with the Constitution of Nepal and has a fixed annual timeframe for 15 days of the Nepali month Jyestha (generally falls during May/June) to consult with stakeholders. The project will also ensure that public consultations are conducted at all levels of the government and among the concerned governmental entities.

The Ministry of Finance sets budget ceilings and negotiates with the respective line Ministry. There are no delays in the implementation process, as the Project Implementation Unit (PIU) is established by the Objection Letter from the Government of Nepal ensures that the project credibility and the government commitment.

AEPC will perform both functions of Accredited Entity (AE) and Executive Entity (EE) and will have a separate Project Implementation Unit to execute the project. A dedicated Project Implementation Unit will be set up at AEPC, and it will be guided by a Project Management Committee. Four Provincial Project Management Unit will be established with two (one technical and one coordinator staff) for each unit. The each unit will be responsible for implementing the project in their respective province.

The project will engage with private sector, as a vendor, to provide CCS technologies and services, whereas Local Governments will play an important role in planning, demand collection, awareness raising by providing information on the project, and other related activities.

The project will ensure a just and inclusive energy transition. It maintains the gender and equity aspect by uplifting the women of Terai Region to ensure their involvement in the technical and community outreach activities related to the project. The project aims to provide skill development training for women and men from the target communities, women group, single women to capacitate them in repair and maintenance of the cook stoves.

Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation (MoEWRI): The MoEWRI is the line ministry of AEPC. It will be responsible for providing strategic guidance and coordination among various partners and stakeholders. MoEWRI will review and approve the Standard Operating Procedure prepared by AEPC for project implementation. It provides strategic guidance and coordination among various partners and stakeholders.

Compliance and Ethics Subcommittee: AEPC has a Compliance and Ethics Subcommittee, which provides complete oversight functions and ensures accountability of the project.

Project Management Committee: The committee will be chaired by AEPC Executive Director and its membership will include the head of each department of AEPC; and representatives from private sectors and technical institutions such as NAST and RETS. Member secretary will be the head of a


---
**Page 39**
---

particular department of AEPC that manages the project. The management committee will ensure the collaboration among the partners and stakeholders that involve in project implementation.

Project Implementation Unit (AEPc): A dedicated PIU will be established within AEPC to oversee and manage the project. Under strategic guidance of the project management committee, PIU will be responsible for day-to-day management and monitoring of the project activities according to approved annual work plans. The PIU will also ensure that all project activities are in line with the overall project objectives and after completion of each activity, it will submit a progress report to AEPC. The PIU will also engage private service providers to provide services to the project beneficiaries in installation of CCS and after commissioning of the technologies. The PIU will develop a detailed work plan and monitoring framework for the full project duration and get approval from competent authority. Further, PIU will develop a procurement strategy for all project activities including the selection of service providers that outline the detailed implementation procedure of the project including roles of all responsible institutions and project stakeholders, including in terms of procurement, monitoring, reporting and auditing. The project will also ensure compliance with the relevant laws and regulations of Government of Nepal's rules and procedures regarding the acquisition of project services, supplies, and works.

Provincial Project Management Unit: The project area touches six Provinces of Nepal, however to optimize the resource allocation, only four Provincial project management unit will be established that shall coordinate with AEPC. Each provincial project management unit will have a technical officer stationed at each provincial project management unit. The technical officer will look after the technical issues, support service providers and the vendors, and the CCS coordinator will oversee the activities and coordination of the project in the respective province. Provincial Governments will be responsible for providing necessary resources to implement the project activities within their provinces but will also support nearby LGs of adjacent provinces. Provincial Governments will be instrumental in supporting AEPC in implementing the project at provincial level.

Local Government Unit (LG): Municipalities/Rural Municipalities: Local Government plays a very important role in the project implementation. AEPC will identify at least 150 LGs for the deployment of CCS, where LGs would also contribute partially in funding the technologies to their respective households. Local Governments will be responsible for providing necessary resources and support to implement the project awareness on benefits of CCS and monitoring of the progress project. At LG level, the project will allocate at least one CCS mobilizer in each local government They will collaborate with their respective LGs for the implementation of the project activities. The LGs will also provide necessary resources. Overall, LGs will be instrumental in supporting AEPC to implement the project activities at the ground level.

Vendors: Vendors contracted through bulk tendering and reverse auctioning process as per PPP/APPRE, will be responsible for providing services on CCS, particularly the distribution and installation of the CCS technologies. The vendors who have successfully bid for the supply of CCS technologies in the bulk tendering process will participate in tender calls of AEPC. The selected vendors then in coordination with LGs will set-up distribution and installation of the technologies as per the local demand. The supply chain will be formed in coordination with AEPC and LGs.

# Internal and Third Party Monitoring Agency: Direct monitoring of delivery of CCS will be done by an internal monitoring mechanism formed at LGs level. An independent third party monitoring agency will be hired for outcome and impact monitoring. The third party will specifically assess and verify appropriate implementation of the project activities, including:

- The technical parameters of the CCS technologies.
- Quality and standard of the CCS technologies.
- Migration of beneficiaries to use cow dung, dung cake and LPG and
- Adaptation benefits in terms of vulnerable communities and remote areas, among others.

## Project Beneficiaries: The project beneficiaries are the ultimate target groups of the project and will receive services from private service providers and LGs on CCS technologies. Further, the beneficiaries will receive training to use cow dung as a fertilizer for increasing agricultural productivity, reduce vulnerability and build resilience to climate change impact through the time saving, health benefits, gender empowerment and increase in agricultural productivity by using cow dung as fertilizer, by replacing its use for cooking fuel. Further details of the project beneficiaries and the selection criteria have been defined in section B.3 Project and Program Description.

AEPC will call tender for each CCS technology and then select a vendor for bulk purchase. An advance payment, not exceeding 20% of the contract value, is to be tendered to the vendor against bank guarantees per annum. Additionally, the vendor must submit performance guarantees equal to at least 5% of the contract value as per the PPAPPR prior to the contract signing. The performance guarantee will be tied to the quality of the technologies and covers all the technologies ensuring after sales services in the form of guarantees and warranties for the period of one year (defect liability period). The advance bank guarantee has to be provided by the vendor and will be tied to the quality of the technologies and covers all the technologies ensuring after sales services in the form of guarantees and warranty for the period of one year. After the CCS technology will be deployed, the total amount of 80% of the contract value will be paid to the vendor. The remaining 20% of the contract value, AEPC will hold 40% of the final payment, which will only be paid after the independent third party monitoring and verification. The equity from the beneficiaries will form part of the technology payment. The portion of the equity that is not required to be provided by the beneficiary will be provided by the private sector after installation of the given technologies. The equity portion from the beneficiaries will be provided to the private sector after installation of the given technologies. The equity from the beneficiaries is not reflected in the total budget of the project.

Describe the experience and track record of the AE and EE with respect to the activities (sector and country/region) that they are expected to undertake in the proposed project/programme

AEPC, under Ministry of Energy, Water Resource and Irrigation, is the national focal agency for promotion of renewable energy technologies in Nepal. They have been implementing different programmes/projects related to renewable energy technologies with a view towards promoting renewable energy technologies and achieving the objectives of the United Nations “Sustainable Development Goals” (SDGs) and “Sustainable Energy for All” (SE4ALL) initiatives while reducing dependence on traditional fossil fuels, thereby improving access to affordable clean energy to improve the livelihoods of people and create employment opportunities, especially in rural areas.

AEPC is also a technical arm of the Ministry on climate change related activities. Established in 1996, AEPC was set up to promote the use of alternative/renewable energy technologies to meet the energy needs in Nepal. Mandated as the focal agency in the area of renewable promotion and energy


---
**Page 43**
---

efficiency. APEC’s activities include renewable energy policy formulation and development, and implementation of projects. APEC has implemented projects and programmes in the area of Climate change mitigation and adaptation, RE promotion and Low carbon economic development worth more than $285 Million USD up until 2017.

APEC has implemented several programs and projects in coordination various development partners including: World Bank, UNDP, GIZ, DFID, SNV, KfW, etc.
* Rural Energy Development Programme (RDEP) - Phase I, II & III of $33 Million USD in collaboration with World Bank, GIZ, SNV
* Biogas Support Programme (BSP) - Phase III, IV & V of $21 Million USD in collaboration with KfW, SNV
* Energy Sector Assistance Programme (ESAP) - Phase I & II of $48 Million USD in collaboration with Denmark, Norway, KfW, DFID
* Renewable Energy Project (REP) - $30 Million USD in collaboration with EU
* Climate Change Adaptation Project - Phase I & II of 1.54 Million USD in collaboration with DFID, SNV
* KABE Transmission/REES of 1.2 Million USD in collaboration with World Bank and Denmark, Norway, WB, ADB, UNDP, UNCDF, SNV, GIZ, UNESCOP, DFID, KW.

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

Explain why the project/programme requires GCF funding. i.e., Why is the project/programme not currently being financed by public and/or private sector? Which market failure is being addressed with GCF funding?

The GCF funding is crucial in addressing the barriers in CCS sector on the demand and supply side: in terms of awareness, access to finance, regulatory frameworks, quality assurance of electric storages, ICS and biogas, strengthening supply chain, and empowering institutions to create an enabling environment for CCS in long-term.

Government of Nepal investment independently cannot address the climate change mitigation challenges. The government has been investing heavily on fossil fuel-based power generation with carbon emission and climate resilient projects and programmes with its fiscal and monetary policy. On one hand, the financial institutions have been providing loans at a very low interest rates (around 5% to 6%) or zero interest rates, up to 18% by cooperatives**, have been detrimental in expanding CCS market in Nepal.

GCF support will be noteworthy for empowering vulnerable communities: Lower Tier users are willing to switch to Tier 3+ ICS, however they can't afford to pay the capital requirement to switch to more expensive Tier 3+ ICS. They are very poor and most of them are from socially excluded groups. They do not have access to finance or credit facilities. The financial institutions are reluctant to provide loans for CCS technologies called by the financial institutions. Therefore, the project provides financial support for the purchase of Tier 3+ ICS technology which will allow vulnerable user communities to transition to better CCS technologies with high returns.

**GIZ, 2018. Final Report on Gaungyang Municipal Energy Situation of Province 1


---
**Page 44**
---

GCF investment will allow the creation of an inclusive CCS market and a supply chain ecosystem. There is a lack of infrastructure to collect demand and de-rail new investments in renewable energy sector. The existing public companies (or PC companies) has the task to collect demands door to door and invest 100% capital upfront, is not financially motivating and therefore, has not achieved good results. Hence, the proposed bulk tendering, which minimizes the risk of financial loss for the PC companies and reduces the information leak between the vendors and AECP, would alleviate the confidence of investors to move into the CCS market.

Currently there are not many manufacturers and vendors of Tier 3+ CCS and ECS in Nepal. Moreover, the existing ones are also concentrated in very few urban centers only. There are not many repair and maintenance centres either. There are not many trained skilled human resources. Hence, the GCF support is required to strengthen the supply chain ecosystem, so that the CCS technologies become easily accessible for all stakeholders.

The combined investment support from GCF will help to achieve the GoN national targets in terms of CCS: Current supply delivery mechanism is a very bureaucratic and lengthy process, which makes it difficult for the government to achieve big targets in a short time. Furthermore, prequalified companies are not able to identify the households who need subsidy, and they have not been able to meet the national targets.

GCF support is instrumental in accelerating climate change mitigation and adaptation needs. Under BAU subsidy model, AECP targets to install 20,000 CCS and 25,000 domestic biogas installations as per GoN’s National Action Plan (NAP). This is a separate program for deploying ECS. With this new approach of bulk tendering via revenue auction, AECP can reach 200,000 households annually, which will increase the pace of climate adaptation and mitigation.

Reverse auctioning (RA), ideally, escalates the base competition, which leads to lowered price for buyers. For instance, IEC certified China-manufactured cookstoves has an MRP of 21-22 USD in China. The same product is sold at a price of 35% lower in Nepal due to higher logistics cost and other logistical issues. Further, the retail price for various brands of energy hobs in Nepal ranges from 400-600 NRS. This is because of the high import duty on the product. Reduction in price for the end-user is expected between 15-20%, with no compromise in the quality. Similarly, the Nepalese manufactured Tier 3 ECS having MRP of USD 7743 is reduced to USD 3896 after reverse auctioning. This will increase the adoption rate and end-use pricing as winning bid effectively captures required quality and generates supply base competition.

The Project emphasizes increasing awareness for behavioral change that will lead to an increase in demands for CCS, which will cater to increase in investment from the private and banking sectors. GoN and beneficiaries will share about 60% of the cost of the project.
