The financing from GCF not only support the implementation of low carbon and climate resilient technologies in every household/cooking of Nepal but also by introducing an avant-garde approach to mainstreaming energy sector output based financing through the energy MIS and mainstreaming LGs role in the renewable energy. This will create a massive change in the business-as-usual process of financing renewable energy technologies in Nepal and the government will be able to scale up its efforts at all the country.

The project involves low risk and yet yields high returns in terms of economic benefits for GCF in the form cost saved for cooking for beneficiaries, reduced carbon emissions, improved health and time saved for beneficiaries, as well as reduction in deforestation.


### Explain why the proposed financial instruments were selected in light of the proposed activities and the overall financing package. i.e. What is the coherence between activities financed by grants and those financed through co-financing? How does this selection reflect the principle that the co-financing is concessionally of the GCF financing compared to that of the co-financing? If applicable, provide a short market read on the prevailing of the pricing and/or financial markets for similar projects/programmes.


### With regards to the selection of the financing instrument, during an activity related to the rehabilitation of particular technologies, what was the initial approach and how did it evolve into the current one? Explain in detail. What lessons were learned from this small scale experience with the bulk tendering process. The process though had proven beneficial, it could not be replicated due its nature associated with disaster risk recovery. Bulk tendering needs to be mainstreamed in Nepal and will be done in line with the Public Procurement ActRegulation (PPAARR) of the country. Hence for the alignment with the PPAAPPR, reverse auctioning aligns with the least cost based selection process. Least cost based selection process will ultimately ensure that the technology is procured from the lowest bidder. This will create a competitive environment that will result in better valued products attracting more consumers to adopt CCS.


### Both of the processes were chosen to ensure creation of a market place for the vendors and better control over the quality of the output. The output based payment approach ensures that only the best product will be procured at a singular cost. The marketing for the vendor is done by the government which means no additional overheads for the vendors. The output based payment approach ensures the cost recovery, as well as the risk mitigation and sustainability of the proposed technologies. Furthermore, the successful implementation of the financing instrument will result in a replication of the process in other technologies such as solar home systems and water supply.

This will also help to meet the country’s needs, creating a market and supply chain ecosystem, mitigating the risk of implementing an innovative

approach, implementing innovative approaches of demand aggregation and mainstreaming LGs proposed in the project. The remaining GAP identified was $2 million dollars. The internal capacity of AEPC can absorb this grant without stressing the internal working mechanisms.

This level of concessionality is also the minimum required for ensuring project sustainability and achieving additional fiscal leverage for promotion of electric and Tier 3+ cook stoves.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 45 OF 113

The project involves low risk and yet yields high return for GCF investments. GoN will be supporting roughly 60% of the total cost as grant and equity. This model uses the use of gas as a cost reduction measure, with the remaining 40% to be financed using LDCF/GRF funds and ICS technology. Vendors who bid for bulk tendering will need a bank guarantee that provides a risk mitigation mechanism in case of non-performance. The project is structured into three phases: the payment method is scheduled in three tranches with one year time frame for vendors who provide the technology, and two years for the management on behalf of AEPC, while ensuring quality assurances and project implementation guarantees.

In terms of beneficiaries, the project will capacitate the local service centers and trained them to ensure sustainability in the long term. Initially a market will be created by the project and subsequently the market will grow as more households adopt the technology. The target beneficiaries are low income sectors including hydroponic, solar and clean cooking solution. Hydroponic development still for a prototype phase with 100 units of plants to be produced in the first year. This is followed by an initial financing push which has proven to sustain itself in the long run. As per our Financial and Economic Analysis, the cost of the technology will be reduced over time as more households adopt it.

The market for solar cookers has been taken as the monthly cost fuel wood for TCS and ICS; in practice very few households actually buy firewood in rural areas of Nepal, and most of the households collect it rather than buying from the market. The cost of cooking with firewood is also not a major concern to the beneficiaries. The ECS beneficiaries usually source firewood from community forests free of cost. Therefore, a meager figure has been assumed for this cost. It should be noted that the beneficiaries are aware and conscious about sustainability of the project since beneficiaries are aware of the health and economic benefits of the Tier 3 in comparison to TCS.

The investment from the individuals though not reflected in the total budget of the project, however we are expecting contribution ranging from 5% to 20% from the users based on the type of technology. The Tier 3+ ICS is where we expect low user contribution as these are targeted towards the vulnerable communities and those who cannot afford to pay for the technology. This will be a significant user contribution.

The barriers to investment and indebtedness will not pose a major concern since the cost of the technology is very affordable compared to other alternatives available in the market. The cost of ECS which will be borne by the beneficiaries has been considered in the Financial Analysis. An upfront cost of US$ 67 (NPR 2000) has been considered that will include all basic utensils required by a typical household for cooking and eating. This is a very affordable price compared to other alternatives, which will have capacity to purchase the utensil.

## B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Explain how the project/programme sustainability (financial, institutional, social, gender equality, environmental) will be ensured in the long run after project closure, including how the project's results and benefits are expected to continue.

In terms of the electric cookstoves targeted to the most vulnerable with an annual income ranging between USD 250 to USD 2000, we have performed sensitivity analysis considering various scenarios based on the requirement and cost at the beneficiary level. Taking it to the consideration that the households will be able to pay for the technology in a phased manner, our financial and institutional analysis shows an IRR of 39% with payback period of 2.15 years without external interventions. Furthermore, taking into account the fact that the beneficiaries are aware of the health benefits along with electricity consumption our financial analysis shows IRR of 3% with payback period of 4.57 years without external intervention. Here the cost benefit analysis definitely shows financial sustainability post


---
**Page 48**
---

project implementation compared to the existing LPG used for cooking. In terms of Tier 3+CS targeted for the poor rural households of the country, the decrease in fuel-wood consumption by 30% compared to traditional cookstoves and open burning would generate positive cash flow from the very first year to the beneficiaries. The project will continue after the completion of the proposed CCS project. In addition to the additional project benefits of improved indoor air quality and with more than 50% time saved on fuel collection, the project will also contribute towards reducing deforestation and carbon emissions. It is expected that the project will contribute towards clean cooking technologies. Furthermore, the project with the implementation of reverse auctioning for LPG cylinders and biogas digesters will be able to provide a market price for LPG cylinders as it will be available in the market at an average rate USD 38.00 on technology and biogas at an average rate of USD 15.00 per month. The project will also ensure that the cost of cooking gas is reduced by providing provision to import ICS raw materials/accessories for ICS and Biogas accessories with custom duty discounted to 1%. In 2020, GDN has retained custom charges in induction cookstoves from 15% to 3%, which will further reduce the cost of cooking gas. The project will also ensure that the price of LPG cylinders is reduced at 1% in case of ECS. The national budget for fiscal year 202/2022 indicates an addition of 15/29 MW of electricity generation capacity from renewable energy sources, which will be available to meet the demand of Boesheki and small and medium projects promoted by private investors. In addition to the import provision of ICS raw materials/accessories for ICS and Biogas accessories with custom duty discounted tariff incentives with arrangement of free electricity to households consuming up to 20 units of electricity and 50% discount on electricity consumption above that limit, the project will also ensure that the subsidies and cost benefit analysis ensures the financial sustainability of the project. AEPCL will further work with the government to ensure that the price of LPG cylinders is reduced at 1%. The project will facilitate transition from LPG to ECS. LPG is the third most imported item by Nepal with about $300 million dollars a year, which is not only harmful for health but also cooking reduce trade deficit and also provide energy security for Nepal, thus directly contributing to both economic and social development.

The proposed CCS project is designed with close coordination and consultations with relevant government stakeholders to ensure ownership and sustainability of the project. The project will continue after CCS project completion as it will be able to generate positive cash flow from the very first year to the beneficiaries. The project will also contribute towards reducing deforestation and carbon emissions by promoting of biogas, ICS, gasifier, bio briquettes and ECS. Likewise, Nepal is a signatory of SDGs, which envisions CCS for all by 2030. The White Paper 2018 prepared by the Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation (MoEWRI) has projected that the country will have to import about 15/29 MW of electricity from renewable energy sources by 2024, out of which 10,000 MWh would be consumed internally, justifying government’s decision for CCS project. The project will also ensure that the price of LPG cylinders is reduced at consumer base that will ultimately create a conducive environment for electricity consumption at affordable cost and thus contribute towards reducing trade deficit. In addition to the subsidies and programs set by the government, surcharge on adoption of electric cooking would grow, as reliability and affordability of electricity supply gets better.

The project will also ensure that the production of Tier 3+ ICS, Biogas, ECS and pellets. The Stoves/Fuel will be produced locally using local work force and materials, which is an important factor for long-term sustainability of this project. Besides, the project will prioritize multi fuel type Tier 3+ ICS that uses locally


---

available materials. Technical assistance and capacity development component will help beneficiaries to produce the fuel locally and mobilize community managed forest user groups to bring down the cost of raw materials such as biomass, woodchip, husk, etc. The project will also help in reducing production cost of TCS and LPG.

The project will address existing barriers in promotion of CCS to poorest households by improving access for vulnerable communities and providing support and training on repair and maintenance of proposed technologies. This will be achieved through the establishment of a Project Management Unit (PMU) at the project level. There are several local NGOs working in clean cooking sector. The project would strengthen and increase the number of offer sales service centres, which will result in quicker access for CCS users. This will be achieved through establishment of a PMU at the project level and after sales services. Accessible and affordable CCS options with well-developed supply chain and after sales service would not only increase the CCS usage, but also minimize the re-fuelling effect to TCS and LPG.

One of the major components of the project is to capacitate local and provincial governments to prioritize CCS generation in their periodic and annual planning, formulating appropriate policies and mechanisms for promotion of CCS. The PMU will be established within the Provincial Energy Division’s Energy Branch. The PMU will be a joint effort of the Government and the GCF. Hence, it will help in building required capacities, expertise, human resources, budget allocation and support directly in development of MEP for prioritizing CCS at local level perpetually.

The project will create a conducive environment in the CCS sector which ensures that bank and other financial institutions are willing to provide financing for CCS projects. The project explores opportunities to invest in the CCS sector during and post the project cycle, enhances long-term sustainability of the project. In the case of TCS, it is expected that the project will lead to a market led and enabled approach in the CCS sector coupled with conducive policies and mechanisms which will ensure continuous supply of raw materials, efficient production process and extensive use of proposed technologies. In addition, other ongoing energy related development projects are also expected to be linked directly or indirectly with the proposed CCS technologies by providing access to finance, e.g. via low interest rate loan and cash fund. This project is aligned with the GoN's vision to implement renewable energy and energy efficiency projects for climate change mitigation with multiple co-benefits involving Vendors, PPGs, LCGs, Adopters, and other stakeholders. The project will replace 100,000 old refrigerators with GCS technologies; 490,000 Tier-3 ICS; 10,000 Household Biogas, and 500,000 CCS respectively. As a result, the project will potentially reduce greenhouse gas emission by about 6.51 million tons of CO2e during the lifetime of the technologies. The project will also contribute to climate change adaptation through energy efficiency by replacing LPG cylinders with ECS. programme investment. Describe the following, if applicable:

Paradigm Shift in public procurement process: The project introduces concept of bulk tendering via reverse auctioning, along with an output based financing model. Bulk tendering is a relatively new concept in the context of public procurement of Nepal, especially when the tendering involves demands aggregating across multiple government agencies. This approach will significantly reduce the cost of CCGS technologies. The output based financing method, thereby significantly reducing the cost of CCS technologies. The output based financing mechanism will increase transparency by ensuring that the underlying multiple check and balance mechanisms ensure quality assurance and de-risk project investments.

The bulk tendering through reverse auctioning model, coupled with output based financing, will transcend from the BAU subsidy delivery mechanism of GoN, creating a paradigm shift in the public procurement process that could be adopted by other government agencies too.

Paradigm Shift in implementation of technologies: The specification of these technologies will be provided to the project implementing agency for their activities under output 2.1, "Enhancing product standards, conducting assessments, surveys and analysis". The project, during its implementation phase, will also promote the right technology. The project will ensure that all products are available according to and meet the national standards as defined in the Nepal International Bureau for Solid Biomass Cookstoves (NIBS, 2016). AEPC currently promotes biogas plants of different capacity, such as 3 to 5 m³/day, 8-10 m³/day, and up to 10 m³/day, and the project mainstreams the innovative GGC 204T and modified GCC 204T designs.

Additionally, the project will also mainstream the use of Tier 3+cookstoves (e.g. Forced Draft cookstove) and surge its penetration in the market, dominated by TCS. The cookstoves that the project intends to displace are mainly Tier 1 and Tier 2 cookstoves which have a high carbon footprint (up to 40 kg CO₂eq per year), high PM emission to less than 4 mg/MJU) and increased safety factors (greater than 95 Johnshons).

Furthermore, the Nepal Bureau of Standard and Metrology (NBSTM) recently released standards for safety and performance for domestic electric appliances, including electric cookstoves. The project will develop a technical guidance document on the implementation of these standards. The Renewable Energy Testing Station (RESTS), the government agency, is aggressively working on enlisting its testing to implement testing procedure set by NBSTM and IEC on electric cookstoves. In consequence, this will also increase the capacity of RESTS in terms of testing and certification for electric cookstoves. The project will support these institutions in preparation of the technical guidelines and standards for the given technologies.

As such, the consequence and spillover the project creates will help paradigm shift in adopting new technologies and novel standardization. This will surge the penetration of electric cookstoves in the market, nudge the local manufacture and production, and catalyze the standardization and testing

## Scaling up of CCS Technology: The project has huge potential for scaling up as this aligns with the GoN aim of attaining Clean Cooking Solutions for all by 2030. The project meets 98% target of the Second NDC of Nepal which aims to install 500,000 improved cookstoves, specifically in rural areas by 2025. Furthermore, as per the Second NDC of Nepal, currently around 5% of households use electric induction stoves and this is expected to increase to 30% by 2030. The project contributes significantly to the achieve the target on the use of electric stoves. Strengthened supply chain, expansion of the manufacturing base in the country, will project, will proliferate across the country without external interventions even after the project period.

## Graduation from TCS to Tier 3+ ICS is an innovative approach and a game changer for Nepal and many other high-uptake countries. Electric cooking is a segment that has remained underutilized relatively at times, as its adoption is very low. The shift towards electric cooking with the government aspiration of generating surplus electricity from hydropower will bring in the next generation cookstoves to the market.

In the areas where the country LPG and biomass are being used extensively, it will transform the energy usage pattern of households creating an environment where the future generations will easily adopt CCS as a primary means of cooking. The financial and economic benefits coupled with import incentives for electric stoves will further encourage the adoption of electric cooking and help the users shift from the traditional way of cooking. The projects enables demand creation and ensures a market led approach with improved reliability of the proposed technologies with standards and guidelines in place. This will be achieved through the local level value chain with extensive campaigns and promotional activities at the community level.

## Contribution to CCS policies at sub-national level: Mainstreaming the LGs, this project aims to support in developing municipal energy plans and the overall project implementation. This will empower the LGs as they will have a clear roadmap for implementing CCS projects. The LGs will be able to reach out more LGs reaching out APEC for technical support to develop MEPA and implement CCS deployment programmes. Major shifts will be seen across the LGs with their capacities enhanced in identifying available opportunities, developing plans and implementing CCS projects in their energy mix with a focus gained on CCS.

## Potential for knowledge generation and sharing: The project has potential for knowledge generation and sharing as it is providing technical knowledge on bulk lending, reversing auctioning and output based financing. Further, the use of a management information system, where data will be available from the stage of energy plans, demand collection to project implementation, can be utilized to assess the demands and reach of CCS projects.

LGs and local communities will learn a lot from the use of CCS as the project will create awareness on and access to CCS, inform the vulnerable people of benefits in terms of health and efficient systems. The project will hence increase the knowledge of Local Governments and the stakeholders about the impacts that efficient technologies can have on their daily lives.

## D3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Describe the wider benefits and priorities of the project/programme in relation to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and provide an estimation of the impact potential in terms of:

- Environmental co-benefits
- Social co-benefits including health impacts
- Economic co-benefits
- Gender-sensitive development impact

The use of modern, efficient and clean cooking solutions will result in numerous sustainable development benefits. The project will contribute to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), such as reducing indoor air pollution by 30% (SDG 11) and improving access to affordable and clean energy for drug-women in collecting wood fuel (SDG 5). Improving access to affordable and clean energy (SDG 7) and reducing GHG emissions and strengthening the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change (SDG 13). The project will also help in reducing pressure on the forest, thus conserving the forest resources (SDG 15).

#### Environmental co-benefits: reduction of indoor air pollution and improve air quality, and reduction of forest degradation

The project will contribute to a reduction of indoor pollution and improve air quality. On average, Tier 3+ CTS will reduce indoor PM2.5 concentration by 30%, and biogas and ECS will completely replace the use of fuel wood, thus reducing deforestation and forest degradation. This will help to conserve fragile ecosystem of Churia, which is a primary source of timber, fuel wood, fodder and other forest products for downstream communities.

In addition, the amount of cow dung replaced through Tier 3+ ECS can be used as organic manure, thus improving soil quality. Besides, using cow dung in domestic biogas plants will help to mitigate carbon emissions by converting methane into carbon dioxide.

#### Social co-benefits including health impacts: increased resilience through improved health

The use of clean cooking solutions promoted by this project will contribute in reduction of health risk caused by indoor air pollution. The project will also reduce the number of deaths and illnesses, especially women and children. In total, more than 30% of reduction in indoor air pollution is expected by replacing the TCS with Tier 3+ ECS. WHO estimates the death of 8,700 people per year due to indoor air pollution in Nepal A recent study shows the prevalence of productive cough, tearing of eyes, difficulty


---
**Page 60**
---

breathing, asthma, headache within cooking member of family who are involved in more than five years in kitchen**1**. Introduction of CCS will help in reducing such adverse health impacts and in reducing deaths caused by indoor air pollution.

Economic co-benefits: job creation, increased revenue from cost savings, time gained for income-generating activities and education, and country's saving from LPG replacement

Approximately, 2,400 people will be trained during the project period. A total of 1,200 trainings will be provided to local technicians, mechanics, and installers in the use of CCS technologies. The training centers and metal workshops can capitalize them in providing after sales service, domestic production of CCS technologies, pellets and briquettes, as well as maintaining the supply chain. In addition, 1,200 trainings will be provided to local technicians, mechanics, and installers on the use of ICSs. The training centers and metal workshops can capitalize them in providing after sales service, domestic production of CCS technologies, installation, electrical safety and house wiring to CCS beneficiaries and vulnerable and displaced persons. The training centers and metal workshops can also capitalize them in providing logistics, broaching installation, electrical safety and house wiring to CCS beneficiaries and vulnerable and displaced persons. The trainings will be provided by the project team and local trainers. Technicians and installers will create the much needed skilled human resources for the sector. The project would directly employ about 170 staffs, whereas it would create employment opportunities for approximately 2000people/annually.

Cost for fuel wood can be saved in households where fuel wood is purchased rather than collected. These savings can be used in livelihood enhancing activities such as education or for starting small businesses. In households where wood is fuelled, the time spent on wood collection can be saved and utilized to engage in other income generating activities. The cost of fertilizers can also be reduced where fertilizer needs to be purchased; the cost for fertilizers can be saved by using cow dung as fertilizer.

The LPG replacement will nurture the internal consumption of clean energy in line with the hydropower potential of the country and should significantly reduce the government's trade deficit**2**.

Gender-sensitive development impact: reduced workload and exposure to indoor air pollution, time gained for income-generating activities and education, training and employment to women, impacts on revising traditional gender norms

The project enhances women’s access and entitlement to benefits. Since the women have been the direct user of the cook stoves, it will benefit women by reducing their exposure to indoor air pollution, thereby improving their health status. The study conducted in 2015 by the ECDI (Energia, 2015) has survey output of male beginning to participate in cooking and preparing snacks after the introduction of cookstoves in their households. This is changing gender norms of our society, which was traditionally dominated by women. The project will also reduce the workload of women to collect fuel wood, resulting in time saving that they can use for other productive activities such as getting an education, an employment or care for children or socialize, as per their preferences**3**. More than 50% of the time will be saved via the proposed CCS. Further, the project

In Nepal, about 73.5% of households use wood fuel (such as dung or agri waste) as their primary fuel source to cook food. This inefficient type of cook stove has resulted in increased greenhouse emissions, indoor air pollution and put pressure on the forest. The WHO has estimated 6,700 deaths a year from indoor air pollution in Nepal. Apart from biomass, a large portion of urban residents use LPG for cooking. However, the cost of LPG is very high compared to wood fuel. In addition, LPG based cooking is a huge economic problem for Nepal stressing its budget deficit.

The TCS users are the most vulnerable community as they are among the poorest and socially excluded groups. 23.4% of the population in the Terai region survives under absolute poverty. According this, 33 % of the poor households in the Terai region use wood fuel for cooking (100% of the Tharu, 25% of the Indigenous group) and 38.2% Dalt population of Tarai live under extreme poverty[1].

In Nepal, cooking is almost entirely carried out by women, requiring on average about four hours a day time compared to all other daily household chores.

The per capita wood stove at US $ 1,004 (Rs 103,353) in the fiscal year 2018/19 and the main portion of income goes for sustaining livelihoods. In Nepal, average achieved a score of 0.148 according to the

Please describe how the beneficiary country takes ownership of and implements the funded programmes/programmes. Describe the following:

GoN has endorsed Investment Prospects and Biomass Energy Strategy in 2017, with aim of attaining Clean Cooking Solutions for all in two stages: by improving solid fuel use by 2025 and by 2030 traditional way of cooking will be replaced. The project supports Nepal's Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) of reducing 90% fossil fuel dependency by 2050 and equipping households in rural areas with smokeless kitchen. Approved by the NDA, the project is aligned to GoN’s Investment Prospects and Biomass Energy Strategy and Sustainable Approach Paper for 15th Periodic Plan (2019/20 to 2023/24) that gives high priority to household biogas, ICs, and production of bio-briquette and other renewable energy.

The climate change policy 2019 aims to contribute to socio-economic prosperity of the nation by building a climate-resilient economy. The policy has been introduced with the objective of providing policy guidance to various levels and thematic area to develop resilient society by reducing risk of climate change impacts. The policy covers eight thematic areas, namely: Agriculture and Food Security, Forest Biodiversity and Watershed Conservation, Water Resource and Energy, Rural and Urban Habitats, Industry Transport and Physical Infrastructure, Tourism and Culture, Health and Education, Disaster Risk Reduction and Management, Inter-thermaic areas (Gender Equality and Social Inclusive Livelihoods and Good Governance, Awareness Raising and Capacity Development, Research, Technology Development and Exploitation, etc.). The policy also aims to enhance climate change adaptation capacity of persons, families, groups and communities vulnerable to it.

and at risk of climate change, mobilize national and international financial resources for climate change mitigation and adaptation in equitable and just manner; mainstream gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) into climate change mitigation and adaptation programmes. The Ministry of Finance (GoF) is the National Designated Authority (NDA) under the Green Climate Fund. Nepal aims to achieve its climate action beyond NDCs and proactively and systematically builds the national capacity at different levels to ensure that the knowledge and processes related to GCF mechanisms are transferred to diverse stakeholders and contribute to sustainable capacity building efforts.

The role of Nepal NDA is as follows:

1. Provide broad strategic oversight that is aligned to national priorities for developing the GCF country programme and communicating strategic country priorities to access GCF funding.
2. Convene a multi-stakeholder platform to ensure effective coordination, accountability, transparency, buy-in, ownership, and inclusiveness of all stakeholders in the process.
3. Facilitate the accreditation process for national institutions and provide 'Nomination Letters' to facilitate their participation in the private sector and civil society organizations for their application for the accreditation status under the GCF.
4. Facilitate the development of GCF funding proposals and the implementation of GCF funded projects in Nepal by engaging with accredited entities to align funding proposals with national strategic priorities.
5. Provide leadership on the deployment of readiness and preparatory support funding in Nepal.

## D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe how the financial structure is adequate and reasonable in order to achieve the proposal's objectives, including addressing existing bottlenecks and/or barriers, and providing the minimum concessionary funding required for the project to be implemented with other public investments. Refer to Section B.5 on the justification of GCF funding requested as necessary.

Nepal has not been able to effectively expect climate finance for its low carbon and climate resilient programmes. There is also lack of appropriate financing mechanism to incentivize vendors and de-risk their investment in CCS technologies.

The existing BAU subsidies model, where in the vendors have been carrying out the task of collecting demands door to door, and taking all financial risk by putting 100% capital upfront, has failed to drive enough participation from the vendors to achieve the CCS installation targets set by AEPC. The fund from GCF will be used for providing a low interest loan to the vendors with an option of equity guarantees, which would allow them to use leverage financing to install more CCS technologies, thus increasing the CCS deployment than in BAU scenario.

On the institutional side, the project should set up model by introducing an avant-garde implementation mechanism that can provide output-based outcome financing, renewable energy MIS and mainstreaming LGUs role in the renewable energy. This will create a massive change in the business as usual process of financing renewable energy technologies in Nepal and the government have a tried and tested model that can be replicated.

GCF grant will support achieving national targets and Nepal's NDCs while cutting the usage of fossil based LPG and fuel wood for cooking. For a country which aims to generate surplus electricity in the near future and the residential sector as the primary consumer of energy in the country, proliferation of

## ECS will be a tipping point in creating a paradigm shift in the overall clean energy consumption scenario.

Successful implementation of the project could allow the replacement of LPG at a larger scale in the country. Bringing additional tiers in the sector, the GCF grant will be utilized to remove barriers seen in the demand and supply side in terms of awareness, access to finance, regulatory frameworks, and quality assurance of Tier 3+ LCS, biogas and LPG.

The project involves low risk and yet yields high return for GCF investments as AEPC and LGW will be supporting approaches that are conducive for promotion of clean cooking solutions as co-financing.

Appropriate implementation models, specially the reverse auctioning will be adopted to ensure the effective and sustainable operation of the system. Partnering with local governments and financial institutions will further strengthen the efficiency and effectiveness of the project.

The project directly ensures increased involvement of private sector as vendors by the creation of big and continuous market for LCS. The promotion of LCS will also increase awareness among the users which will also ensure market growth of CCS, especially due to promotion of electric cookers.

Please describe the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project/programme, taking into account the total financing and mitigation/ adaptation impact the project/programme aims to achieve, and explain how this compares to an appropriate benchmark.

The project expects to reduce an estimated 6.51 million tons of CO2eq with significant co-benefits in the area of improved health, environment, social, and economy. The total per unit mitigation cost for the project is about $7.54 USD/CO2eq and $3.42 USD/CO2eq of GCF grant. The USD 21.2 million of GCF grants will be combined with USD 28.02 million of co-finance from AEPC and LGW.

The use of the clean cooking solutions provided by this project can contribute to a reduction in health risk caused due to indoor air pollution, thereby improving overall air quality which will primarily benefit women and children. In total, more than 30% of reduction in indoor air pollution is expected by replacing the TCS by Tier 3+ LCS.

The project will be implemented based on the Gender Policy of AEPC and a gender assessment will be provided to ensure that the project will be designed and implemented with due consideration given to benefits by reducing their exposure to indoor air pollution thereby improving their health and increasing earning opportunities.

Please specify the expected economic rate of return based on a comparison of the scenarios with and without the proposed programme.

Calculation of Economic Rate of Return (ERR) is performed both from the project perspective and from the perspective of the beneficiaries and was based on monthly savings in fuel consumption due to transfer of cooking technology, health benefits due to reduction in indoor air pollution and global benefits in carbon emission reduction. The ERR calculation was done from a beneficiary’s perspective considering the uncertainty surrounding technology cost and its associated cost that affects making an informed investing decision at the beneficiary level. Cash outflow from the beneficiary perspective in terms of the usage of utensils, cost of repair and maintenance, annual fuel cost will vary

Upon households. In terms of the electric cookstoves, the analysis takes into considerations the different types and number of electromagnetic utensils required as per the varying food habit and different types of food items that needs to be cooked, the wholesale and retail price point of these utensils and house wiring capacity. The analysis is performed based on the average income level prevalent in the region of the technology. In terms of biogas, the analysis has been performed based on the varying frequency of the repair and maintenance required for the biogas plants. Furthermore, a household income parameter has also been considered to determine the financial viability of the project (based on APEX and OPEX) on the household income based on the different level of household income prevalent in the Terai region. The analysis is performed considering the average income level prevalent in the region without GCF support. In the first scenario GCF support is introduced as grant support to the project i.e. as co-financing and LG's co financing, however, without GCF support the model is based on BAU scenario.

BAU is based on the annual installation target achieved by APECO in FY 2018/19 and assuming the same no. of CCS projects will be implemented every year for the next five years as was done during this year for the next five years. As the CCS projects under APECO so far do not deploy ECS, the BAU scenario does not include it in the analysis. The total budget expended by APECO to conduct CCS related activities that are in line with the proposed project was $4 million in FY 2018/19.

Error in case of inclusion of GCF grant is at 39% as opposed to no returns with a negative NPV of $18.6 million in the BAU scenario.

## 2. Electric Cook Stoves Vs. LPG

Given the need for utensils that are pre-requisite to induction cooking and that the market has a range of products with varied price points, the analysis needs to consider different scenarios which depict that the varying cost of utensils, house wiring, and annual fuel cost and repair/maintenance. Hence, a sensitivity analysis is conducted.

### Sensitivity Analysis Scenario One: (1) Applying a conservative approach wherein households will only purchase a single utensil and cost of house wiring considered when there is a bulk procurement; the cost stands at $33 that needs to be borne by the beneficiaries with (2) Electric cook stoves and NFPs being provided free of charge. (3) Assuming 5% of the CAPEX plus utensils in repair and maintenance as a result of increased usage.

### Sensitivity Analysis Scenario Two: (1) Applying the approach based on varying the food habits that requires different utensils; in this case a stainless steel pot and a 3 litre pressure cooker and the cost of house wiring at retail level, the cost stands at $66.67 that needs to be borne by the beneficiaries with (2) Electric cook stoves and NFPs being provided free of charge. (3) Assuming 5% of the CAPEX plus utensils in repair and maintenance as a result of increased usage.

### Sensitivity Analysis Scenario Three: (1) Further, expanding the range based on varying food habits that requires different utensils; in this case a stainless steel pot, a 5 litre pressure cooker and a frying pan and the cost of house wiring at retail level. (2) Assuming an increase in electric tariff by 10% with increased usage (3) Assuming 10% of the CAPEX plus utensils in repair and maintenance as a result of increased usage.

Please specify the expected financial rate of return with and without the Fund's support to illustrate the need for GCF funding to illustrate overall cost effectiveness.

The financial rate of return (FIRR) from a project perspective for the scenario with CFS is 31% with the NPV of $19,778,226.09 whereas the FIRR for scenarios for BAU has no returns with a negative NPV value of -$54,000,000.

The financial rate of return from this beneficial perspective has been presented in the earlier section.

The reduced returns in both financial and economic analysis for BAU scenario can be cited to the following reasons:

I. Pricing of technologies is done by traditional bidding methods or on a retail basis by the users. This in turns increases the initial CAPEX of CCS projects in the BAU scenario.
II. In addition, the cost of electricity in the country is expected to increase by 10% each year. Although domestic biogas plants provide higher economic benefits in terms of reduced GHG emissions and health benefits in comparison to both the technology it replaces and the technologies that are being proposed for implementation under this project, the OPEX associated with domestic biogas is approximately 5 and 11 times higher than the latter technologies. This increase in financial OPEX renders negative financial returns for BAU scenario.
III. For almost the same amount of funding as in this proposed project, BAU fails to implement electric cooking (ECS) technology which has savings and economic impacts from electric cooking is larger compared to other co-technologies.

Meanwhile, effectiveness of the project without GCF support is an important factor to consider. Due to the resource limitation of AEPIC, the BAU scenario would fail to invest in activities for accelerating climate change mitigation and adaptation measures in the country. This will increase the risk of not mitigating the risk of implementing an innovative approach, implementing innovative approaches of demand aggregation and mainstreaming LGs proposed in the project. Likewise, GCF's support allows promotion of ECS technology which has savings and economic impacts from electric cooking is larger compared to other co-technologies.

The income level and percentage of CAPEX and OPEX on the income level presents a stark picture that beneficiaries will require financial incentive to pursue clean cooking technology. Furthermore, as the requirement of utensils, annual electricity cost and repair/maintenance cost increases as per the different sensitivity analysis, the need to provide financial incentive to the beneficiaries becomes even more imperative.

It is to be further note that several qualitative factors exist that affects the investment decision from the beneficiary perspective. The following factors need to be taken in to consideration:

* **Vulnerability to disruptions and Energy Security:** As Nepal has no internal supplies of petroleum or natural gas, its vulnerability to disruptions in its supplies of imported fossil fuels is the principal reason why the country is vulnerable to energy security. This is because it is not possible for Nepal to build up national energy security. Energy demand in Nepal has increased for a decade; it has major impact on its economic development and social welfare. The government has been trying to diversify its energy sources but no action was taken in time. Hence, major intervention is required to overcome this present crisis. As per the BBC News on Nepal's forest under threat over fuel crisis50, during the supply constraint of the petroleum product in 2014, the price of firewood increased from NPR. 30 to NPR. 60 per kg. This led to a situation where suddenly increased the demand of firewood. Conservationists said that there was no choice left for the people to cut down trees despite having tradition of protecting the forest. This also led to a situation where in the year 2014, Nepal lost about 50% of its forest cover.

* **Poverty and Inequality:** The technology targets the poorest households of urban and peri-urban areas. The per capita income of the country was USD 1,055 in FY 2019/20 (MoF). Provide 2 houses the largest number of multi-dimensional poor. A separate annex is being prepared taking to considerate the above mentioned points.

* **Remittance:** It is expected that the employment sector will be negatively impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic; and the return of Nepali workers, mainly from Gulf countries, is projected to cause a 20% decline of remittances (CBS, 2020). About 20–25% of the estimated 3 million Nepalese workers abroad are likely to return home, mainly because of two reasons first; the work tenure of about 60 % of


---
**Page 75**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND-FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 73 OF 113

the Nepalese workers is over and, second, a large number of workers have lost their jobs due to the pandemic (Mandal, May 21, 2020). This will affect the inflow of remittance in the country.

Please explain how best available technologies and practices have been considered and applied. If applicable, specify the innovations/modifications/adjustments that are made based on industry best practices.

In collaboration with Nepal Academy of Science and Technology, Renewable Energy Test Stations, AEFC will strengthen the existing testing centers and expand its services at provincial level to determine minimum technical standard and process to approve the products based on the standard. This will ensure dissemination of pre-qualified technologies with internationally accepted standards and parameters.

The project will also provide training to private service providers on standards throughout the project period, set up MIS on testing centre, place and operationalize independent third party monitoring agencies and set up coordination mechanisms at Province and LG level.

* For electric cookstoves: AMS I.C - Thermal energy production with or without electricity, version 21. ## Activity Description Sub-activities Deliverables

### Component 1: Scaling up the deployment of clean cooking technologies through accelerated investment and market development

#### Activity Development Annual Procurement and Deployment plan
**1.1.1:** Development and implementation of annual procurement and deployment plan for CCS technologies

Based on the demand aggregation of each three CCS technologies, a cluster wise deployment procedure will be prepared at regional and provincial level. Different bidding documents for all three CCS technologies will be prepared to procure the technology through bulk tendering via reverse auctioning.

- **Development of cluster wise deployment procedure in accordance with demand aggregation**
  - Preparing bidding documents for procurement of Bulk Tendering via Reverse Auctioning
  - Preparing work plan for Output based financing and third party monitoring and verification

At least three bids for each technology will be called each year.

#### Activity Procurement and Deployment of annual targeted number of CCS
**1.1.2:** Procurement and deployment of annual targeted number of CCS

The project aims to deploy 490,000 Tier-3 Improved cookstoves (ICS), 10,000 Pilot plants, and 500,000 Electric RECS over five years' time.

The procurement of the CCS technologies for this project will follow the bulk tendering via

- **Procurement of CCS through Bulk Tendering via Reverse Auctioning**
  - Testing of parameters adhering to prescribed norms
  - Cluster Based Deployment and Installation of CCS and after sales repair & maintenance service

#490,000 Tier-3+ ICS
#10,000 biogas plant

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Besides the arrangements (e.g. annual performance reports) laid out in AMA, please give a summary of the project/programme specific arrangements for monitoring and evaluation. Please provide the types of internal and external stakeholders involved in the monitoring and evaluation process as well as reporting to the NDA/Focal Point and between AE and Executing Entity (EE) as relevant, identifying reporting obligations from the EE to the AE. This should relate to the frequency of reporting on project indicators, implementation progress and other key performance indicators.

APEC has set up a Monitoring and Quality Assurance (MQA) Unit within the Technical Support Component, which is responsible for setting up an output-based M & E system. One of the major tasks of this unit is to make regular assessment of progress against the set results and annual plans. It will also cover the monitoring of project outputs related to climate change as well as socio-economic impacts including Gender and Social Inclusion (GESI).

The MQA unit has been setting up necessary M&E systems and tools such as baseline, MIS, verification, reporting, review & feedback, progress tracking etc., toward instituting an output-based M&E system. The unit will also be responsible for establishing a mechanism for providing timely feedback mechanism including how it provides management information to different levels of PUI. APEC also envisages developing an integrated web-based tool as another vital instrument so that everyone interested will have easy access to the required information.


---
**Page 98**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 96 OF 113

## The basic building blocks of AEPC monitoring system are (a) quality-controlled tools to collect, process and analyze data (b) processes to structure monitoring cycles in every semester (c) roles and responsibilities describing who does what (d) knowledge management, for example, description of minimum data requirements, FAQs, etc.

The Operations Committee of AEPC having representation of AEPC management and Local Government representatives will hold quarterly meetings to discuss the results and issues involved in the CCS programme. For the household energy component, AEPC will be responsible for day-to-day management and monitoring of the three components' activities and results, which are categorized into two groups:

I. Aggregate Demand Collection Verification and Monitoring

1. AEPC will support Local Governments to strengthen their own demand collection from households, monitoring and evaluation systems to facilitate reporting and quality control.
2. Aggregate demand collection will be collected and categorized for each of the 22 districts and then reported to AEPC.

II. Installation Verification and Monitoring

1. AEPC will conduct lab tests on the quality of Tier 3+ ICS, ECS and biogas during the bulk tendering via reverse auctioning process.
2. AEPC ensures that installation service providers are conducting those activities as per AEPC guidelines and standards set by the monitoring team. During installation of CCS, installation service providers are required to maintain prescribed dimensions of CCS, install serial numbers, follow CCS installation guidelines and ensure proper usage of CCS. After installing CCS, the service provider will provide a one-year after-sales service warranty for the household and a one year after-sales service warranty to customers for repair and maintenance services.
3. Installation service providers are required to send one copy of customer agreement paper to AEPC regional offices. Then the installation service providers will enter the CCS installation data

#### Using a web-based software and submit disbursement requests to APEC.
We will have a GPS designated system with phone number backup in place to track daily installation date of various fields. The option of introducing this system would allow for automatic update of installation data in the database, which can be used by the service provider to generate reports. Using an e-mail and customer satisfaction feedback via text messaging would allow for an easy and cost-effective method for collecting customer feedback, thus ensuring enhanced accountability of the installation service provided.

#### Monthly sales sheets will be communicated to APEC regional offices.
Monitoring officers will revise the sales information, verify and cross-check information, using plausibility checks and random spot checks using GPS tracker and mobile number. After accepting the data, they will be compiled into a monthly report that will include all relevant information such as sales figures and crosschecks. The data then become part of APEC's six-monthly progress reports. These reports inform APEC's board members during their meetings about the current status of target achievement and about areas that need attention.

#### At the provincial/regional level, the project will deploy at least two staff: 1- technical and 1-coordinator.
The technical staff will look after technical issues and supervise service centers and vendors. The coordinator will be responsible for day-to-day management of the project. Under Quality Control under the programme, APEC will establish an inspection team, a call center and a web-based software to keep track of each CCS installed under the programme.

### III. Project Midway Implementation Verification and Evaluation

#### APEC will implement performance status (ISR), audit reports by APEC, interim evaluation and project completion report.
The above-mentioned reports will be compiled as part of monitoring, side memoirs as part of the monitoring and evaluation reports after 24 months of the implementation of the project.

#### Mid-term evaluation/review: an independent mid-term review will be conducted in two and half year of project implementation. It will focus on an overall review of APEC's programmatic approach to CCS deployment, including its institutional set-up and management level of finances.
The mid-term review will also focus on institutional, administrative and organizational set-up of the project. The mid-term review will be based on desk reviews and interviews with key staff and partners. The review will cover the following areas: (i) reduction in the use of traditional biomass as cooking fuel; (ii) improvement in health and safety at work; (iii) increase in the number of households using CCS; (iv) new job creation in the CCS sector; (v) % of HH with continued use of CCS; (vi) impact of awareness campaigns and promotional activities and its impact (vii) effectiveness of the Municipalities' role in promoting CCS deployment at the community level; (viii) switch to the proposed technologies; (ix) reduction in price of CCS with bulk tendering and reverse auctioning and (x) impact of MICS on the overall knowledge management system.

### Impact assessment

1. **Impact assessment**
   - The project will assess the quality of access provided applying scientific methodology by developing a baseline impact assessment test case taking into account the transitional character as well as the complexity of improving access to Clean Cooking Solutions.
   - Improving the cooking situation in the sense of CCS means: considering fuel quality or even switching fuel, improving the design and functionality of stoves, changing cooking practices as well as increasing ventilation and modifying the kitchen. While some internationally discussed measurement approaches are mainly based on stove and laboratory test results, the AEPC will focus on the level of access to cooking energy based on field-based approximations and household surveys.

2. **Further impact studies**
   - Further impact studies will be conducted to assess CCS adoption and daily usage, and the subsequent overall reductions of fuel-wood use and LPG.
     - This will be measured by developing and implementing a survey tool for beneficiaries in the project area.
     - The survey tool is called the Beneficiary Survey Tool (BST), which is a field test used to evaluate CCS performance amongst the beneficiaries. It was designed to assess the impact of the technology on the household, based on the fuel saving results.

3. **AEPC will conduct market development/making scorecards to calculate sustainability of the project**

4. **Project impact monitoring**
   - Project impact monitoring will be performed at the final year of the project period to assess appropriate percentage of the total beneficiaries in terms of mitigation impact through replacement of fuel wood, duing cake and LPG and adoption co-benefit in terms of fuel and time saving.

5. **Regular internal evaluation of the PIU targets against completed work as well as yearly administrative tasks will be performed throughout the project period**

6. **Further socio-economic co-benefits (including gender, income, job creation or health aspects), as well as potential adaptation impacts will be assessed by household survey.**

### Independent Third Party Monitoring and Verification

- AEPC will hire a third party independent agency to monitor and verify CCS technology installed at the households.
  - This third party monitoring and verification would be conducted throughout the project period.
  - They will do their monitoring and verification of the CCS technology against the already defined standard and procedures.

- An independent final evaluation would be conducted within six months after the end of project period. It will focus on overall performance vs. indicators, impacts and finances, and on implementation and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 102 OF 113

## Cost of fuel for tier>3 stove: High High

### Description
Tier >3 ICS requires pellets or very small size wood, meaning the households would have to further process the fuel into wet wood to fit them into the Tier >3 ICS, or they may have to buy pellets or wood chips instead of using the fuel wood they usually collect for free. In either case, this may not result in cost saving or time saving, though emissions savings is there.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will focus on designing the Tier 3+ ICS that uses locally available resources, wood chips and multiple fuel sources. There will be no need to further process the fuel, however may require users' time chip the firewood. For processed fuel, monthly minimum fuel costs are assumed to be lower in tier >3 ICS than that required for Traditional Cookstoves. Since most of the potential beneficiaries of this project are marginalised and poor households, the project will facilitate, provide free or subsidised raw materials for Tier 3+ ICS in particular cases. In addition, the project will provide trainings and technical assistance to lower the fuel production cost.

### Selected Risk Factor 13

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Risk of rebounds | Medium | High |

**Description**

Chances of the beneficiaries of the stoves choose to prefer using old methods over new or underutilization of the stoves.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

Nepal's vulnerability to disruptions in its supplies of imported fossil fuels is the principal reason why the government has been focusing on increasing energy independence. Since electric cooking is a high priority for the Government and the electricity generation in the country is showing a steady growth, further incentives are bound to be rolled out for the promotion of electric cooking. Furthermore, the cost of electricity will continue to decrease with the increase in supply from renewable energy sources. The ECS is more fuel-efficient than LPG and TCS and has lower emissions compared to LPG. As the country is moving towards a steady supply of electricity from the hydropower sector, it is expected that the ECS will be more cost-effective as compared to TCS and ICS. TCS and ICS 3+ are beneficial in terms of annual fuel cost compared to TCS. In addition, the socio-economic and health benefit of ICS 3+ are critical factor that lays as strong foundation for the continued promotion of ECS. The project will ensure that the ECS is adopted by the target users by raising awareness about the cost and other benefits of the technologies with campaigns and promotions. The project will also ensure that the ECS is maintained after project intervention. In addition, scaling up the community based outreach activities through the locally especially women and disadvantaged group as CCS mobilizers and behavior change facilitators will be carried out to ensure that the ECS is adopted by the target users (brochures and new articles). And direct communication with beneficiaries will further mitigate the rebound effect of ECS adoption. The project will also ensure that the ECS is maintained after it has been embedded as different activities in the project. This will further ensure that there remains no gap at the local level including sales, operation and maintenance.

Attach the appropriate assessment and management instruments or other applicable studies, depending on the environmental and social risk category as annexes.

## Environmental and social risk category: AEPC did not identify any significant adverse environmental and social risks. The project is expected to be minor and would come mainly from site-specific impacts related to domestic biogas plants such as management of bio slurry, availability of water resources to operate the biogas plant and the disposal of damaged and obsolete cook stoves.

The project is in principle categorized as C+, as the implementation of the project would have only minor and manageable negative impacts. The accredited entity has provided an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) which addresses potential unintended negative environmental impacts and risks of implementing CCS. The ESMP Institutional arrangement involved in safeguards related to land use, livelihoods, grievance redress mechanism and B & M monitoring. The ESMP also provides a brief description of negative impacts and risks from the project, and have suggested potential mitigation measures.

### Environmental and social risks and impacts

The project focuses on "triple benefits" i.e. they reduce emissions contributing to climate change, provide improved energy security for households and preserve forest and associated ecosystems. Introduction of CCS has a number of social benefits particularly benefiting women. Potential negative environmental impacts from the project implementation are limited to management of bio slurry from biogas plants.

The direct economic and social risks from the project implementation is limited to effective management of bio slurry from biogas plants, disposal of damaged and obsolete cook stoves and availability of water resources to run the biogas plant. If the project is implemented in areas of low water availability, it may lead to travel longer distances to fetch water due to no biogas plant that has a direct impact on women's health as they spend high time burden particularly to women and girls. Measures to avoid the risk are included in ESMP.

### Indigenous people

Ten indigenous nationalities are considered endangered in Nepal, of which 50% are in Terai region. The Majhi community is one such group with a population representing 12% of the Terai population. In Terai, there are different groups and speak Magar, Bajrak, Bhogiur, Awadhi, closely to north Indian dialects and refer themselves as Madhesi, which also makes up the majority in Nepal.

The major beneficiaries of the project are indigenous people as the project aims to enhance their adaptive capacity. Thus, the project is not expected to adversely affect the indigenous peoples or ethnic communities. Instead, the project will bring about positive impacts as it will contribute to resource conservation and sustainable livelihoods for these groups.

### Community health and safety

The project contributes to community health safety by reducing air pollution from using CCS as compared to traditional cooking systems. Use of clean energy sources will reduce the workload of women in collecting fuel wood and leads to better life and improved health conditions. End-user exposure risk, however needs to be monitored through field-testing to ensure that


---
**Page 109**
---

exposure at the household level will not get worse.

## Indirect risks and impacts

There are potential indirect risks and impacts of the project across the industry. The popularity and ease of use of ICS will lead to more fuel and therefore contribute to increasing pressure on fuel wood. This will need to be monitored through the project's impact evaluation that will regularly assess any changes in the fuel wood use over time.

## Stakeholder consultations

In the course of developing the project, stakeholder consultations were held with key stakeholders including government, development sectorial ministries, Supply chain stakeholders, Gender and climate experts, private sector groups, academic and research centers. Through these consultations, a list of issues was identified as well as potential solutions and recommendations for the proposed project. Over 150 people have been consulted through various workshops at national and sub national level. Further Stakeholder engagement activities are embedded in the project implementation including gender equality monitoring and evaluation (GEM) in ESMIP (Annex 4).

## A grievance redress mechanism

For the CCS project has been developed to ensure that complaints are adequately addressed in an effective and timely manner. Anyone who has a complaint or who is negatively impacted by the project is able to communicate their complaints or problems through the official mechanisms of the Government of Nepal (GOI) at the district level. A similar grievance redress mechanism for the project is provided in ESMIP (Annex 4).

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan

The proposal contains a comprehensive and thorough gender assessment, and therefore complies with the operational guidelines of GCCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. It also includes a gender action plan to support implementation of issues identified in the gender assessment.

### The gender assessment was done based on literature reviews, field survey and stakeholder consultations. The study focuses specifically on the impact of the project on the cooking sector with respect to gender. The gender assessment shows Nepal as a country with gender equality issues; however, policies made and in different phases of implementation. Cooking is almost entirely carried out by women while men are more involved in other activities such as farming.

### Women in Nepal spend about 142 minutes to collect the fuel wood whereas men spend only about 50 minutes**. Likewise, the study shows the involvement of women in collecting agricultural residue and cow dung was 70.2% whereas involvement of men is only 29.8%. This shows that in collection of both fuel wood and biomass fuel involvement of women is much more than men.

### In Nepal, on average, there are 18.3 Bhairs (head loads of about 30 kg on average) of fuel wood


---
**Page 110**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WIND PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 188 OF 113

collection per capita per year in Nepal. On average, a household spends 5.0 hours collecting one bhar of fuel wood**. Although there are acts and policies to maintain gender equality but social norms still need to be addressed to achieve equality. APEC has its own Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Policy to improve the lives of women and girls in Nepal. The project will address the issue of reducing the dependency on traditional energy and attain sustainable development through integrating the alternative energy with the socio-economic activities of women and men in the rural communities but implementation is still at a nascent stage.

Gender issues has been inline in most of the energy sector related programmes from the past. Financial privileges are supportive from the policy perspectives, but they haven't been tapped efficiently. Interventions are required to change existing social norms. Political participations are progressive but limited. Gender equality and women empowerment is still a distant dream for many. The GMSI mainstreaming is ongoing in local level and will come into implementation in the subsequent years.

Energy-efficient modern clean cooking solutions (Tier 3+ ICS, biogas, Electric Stoves) have a potential to reduce exposure to smoke related health hazards, reduce the burden on women and children to collect fuel wood, and help them save money (when the full cost of purchase is considered).

The gender analysis indicates that women who spend more time collecting fuel work in a situation to spending much more time engaging other income generating activities, spending more time studying or helping with school work and on entertainment and socializing or engage in the activities they prefer. The project is expected to have major impact on women's lives by providing them with an opportunity to earn money through Tier 3+ ICS to reduce the burden in collecting fuel wood, and provide women with income-earning opportunities. The project will also empower women by training them as Clean Cooking Champions and technically trained women to support maintenance and supply chain, and they will be skilled enough to work in CCS sector even after the project completion. Looking at this, the project has a potential to uplift women in rural Nepal.

The action plan is designed with specific actions to address the issues that have been identified. The gender focal point will be appointed to coordinate the implementation of gender action plan. G3. Financial management and procurement (max 500 words; approximately 1 page)

GoF financial system and procedures will be adopted for this project. In exercise of the power conferred by Section 13 of the Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (Development Committee) Formation Order, 2015, Alternate Energy Promotion Centre is hereby constituted under the Alternative Energy Promotion Centre Financial Discipline and Good Governance Regulation 2018. This regulation assigns


---
**Page 111**
---

responsibility of operation and execution of financial and administrative duties and functions of the AEPF to the Executive Director. This regulation provides authority for AEPF to include grants in its budget.

The regulation assigns duty and responsibility to all the employees of the AEPF to support the Executive Director in the operation and execution of financial and administrative functions. The budgeting and planning of programmes and activities for the fiscal year shall be responsibility of the Executive Director.


Financial and other provisions related to security deposits on activities, projects and programs, fees, service charges, interest earned from investments, income received from grants, loans, contributions, and granting pardons will be based on relevant guidelines and procedures of AEPF under this regulatory framework. In accordance with law, all the income received by AEPF will be deposited into the respective accounts in the bank as per the instructions given by the Executive Director upon receipt of received income, submission of statement of income, ensuring audit and recording of the transactions.


Audit


AEPF will conduct quarterly internal audits in accordance with the Financial General Comptroller Office. The Office of Auditor General may conduct the final audit on any project or the function of any internal and external auditor. Any amount due to AEPF from any donor and kindred as an off-taker, the Executive Director or the respective section head upon receiving such information shall recover the amount and based on the severity of the case may take departmental action.


Being government entity the annual audit of the AEPF is under the jurisdiction of the OAG. In addition, an external audit of the project will be carried out in accordance with Clause 17 of the Accreditation Master Agreement with UCF and internationally recognized accounting standard will be complied to.

Internal Control


The Compliance Unit of AEPF will serve the oversight function to ensure internal control at the institutional level. Compliances and Ethics Sub-committee shall do the oversight of this unit.


Procurement


AEPF will follow the procurement system and procedure of the Government of Nepal: Application of Public Procurement Act (PPA) 2063 and Public Procurement Regulation (PPR) 2064. PPA/PPR will be enforced in all aspects of the procurement processes with regards to securing products and services from any third parties by the Executing Entities (EEs) for funds received from GCF and other sources.


Procurement of goods/services for the EEs generally includes assessing and appointing vendors, contractors or service providers. The AEPF will ensure that all goods and services are procured from responsible vendors or contractors registered in VAT who possess the ability to provide the goods and services on time, quality product and a competitive price. In case of the project, AEPF will fulfill the role of both EAC and EE with distinct institutional setup. This implies that the procurement


---
**Page 112**
---

procedure of the EE will also be governed by the PPA 2063 and PPR 2064. AEPC has also prepared a procurement guideline for executing entities**, 2015 which aligns with PPP/PPR and provides step by step procedure required for procurement of goods, services and works and acts as a simple procedure with following objectives:

- Maximize the value for money.
- Maximum Return of public expenditures in an Economic and Rational manner by promoting competition among service providers.
- Ensure Good Governance by enhancing the managerial capacity of procurement of entities in procuring goods, services and works. This will also ensure that the quality of goods, services, utility services and other services by such entities and by ensuring the same opportunity for producers, sellers, vendors, construction entrepreneurs or service providers to participate in public procurements.

As the Executing Entity is internal to AEPC, Legal agreement between AE and EE is not required. Government of Nepal's financial system, public procurement procedure and fund flow mechanism will be adopted for this project. As an accredited entity, AEPC will receive the fund from GCF as per the Nepal government's financial rules and regulations. In order to ensure good governance in the use of funds, a financial management guideline will be developed for the transparent and effective management of the financial resources. The Project implementation unit will prepare the work plan and budget including a procurement plan and budget. The project implementation unit based on the approved work plan, PNU will mobilize and release the fund according to the agreements/contracts to the service providers.

The procurements will be undertaken by the EE. In terms of the oversight of the procurements. The preventive measures are taken with the applicability of the PPP/PPR for all the procurements made by the EE. The procurement process is also subject to the approval of the Project Management Committee and Ethics Sub-committee and Compliance Unit of the AEPC. Compliance and Ethics Sub-committee is the oversight mechanism initiated at AEPC which directly report to the AEPC Board.

Independent Third Party Monitoring and Verification

AEPC will hire third party independent evaluators after overall deployment draftment is completed, to do the evaluation of the project based on the technical specification, impact, activities, output and outcome. Independent third party evaluators will be their evaluation against the already established standard and procedures which will be linked with the payment system to the service providers. This third party monitoring would be undertaken throughout the project period.

Output Based Financing

The output based financing approach will have several mechanisms set in place. Firstly, the technical specification of the required technologies will be thoroughly tested with the testing centre as entailed by Component 1 of the project. Mobilization advance amount not exceeding 20% will be provided to the vendor against bank guarantee at this stage.

Immediately after the procurement, the technologies will be taken to the respective municipalities. Once the installation is completed and the permission of the respective municipalities and upon the clearance letter from


---
**Page 113**
---

the municipalities, disbursement not exceeding 60% will be provided to the vendor. This disbursement also settles the amount advance provided previously. Hence, at this stage, the vendor will be paid a total amount not exceeding 60%.

Once the third party monitoring is completed and confirmation received from the deployed system, final disbursements of 40% are to be provided to the vendor. Hence, at this stage, the vendor will be paid the 100% of the total advance.

### G.4 Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission of the proposal to the Secretariat. The IDP also states that the confidentiality provisions are subject to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, assignable portions that can be disclosed.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

a) No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal does not include any confidential information. In such a case, the funding proposal and its annexes may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
