# Secretariat’s assessment of FP172

## Proposal name: Mitigating GHG emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean cooking solutions (CCS)

### Accredited entity: Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC)

### Country/ies: Nepal

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

   **Strengths**
   - The project shows a high cost-effectiveness in terms of USD/CtCO2 removed compared with other GCF approved projects and International Energy Agency (IEA) average.
   - The project has high country ownership with support from the Government of Nepal, in addition to directly engaging a minimum of 150 local communities in the region.
   - The project has high co-benefits including its support to the most vulnerable households through improved access to clean cooking solutions as well as an increase in health benefits resulting from reduced indoor air pollution.

   **Points of caution**
   - The project activities will be scaled up in the other regions of Nepal after the project ends, previous country-level support replication is in place. Additional financial support in partnership with the private sector would need to be identified during project implementation.
   - This is the first project the direct access entity (DAE) has submitted to the GCF Board for consideration.

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVIII titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background
- Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change and is ranked as ninth on the Global Climate Risk Index 2020 list of countries most affected by climate change.


---
**Page 118**
---

other two ecological regions: the Hills and the Mountains. Terai is bordered by the Indo-Gangetic plains in the south and the Chhota physiographic region in the north.

4. There is very little use of clean cooking technologies in the Terai. According to the 2011 census, almost 75 per cent of the entire country’s population used traditional biomass as a fuel source (63 per cent in the Terai). In contrast, only 28 per cent of households used liquefied petroleum gas (LPG), whereas, during the same period, 56 per cent of households in the Terai region used LPG. The high prevalence of traditional cooking technologies and continued use of traditional cooking stoves causes health problems and emits a large amount of black carbon and greenhouse gases into the atmosphere. Although there has been some increase in LPG consumption in the Terai has remained fairly constant, LPG consumption is increasing: around 40 per cent of final energy demand in Nepal is met by LPG. The country’s LPG import and sales reported a final increase year 2017/18. Nepal imported and consumed 499,653 tonnes of LPG during the period to be proposed. This has a negative macroeconomic impact in the country because it increases the country’s foreign exchange outflow and puts pressure on supply risk.

5. Nepal relies heavily on hydropower for its electricity generation, which is a clean and renewable energy source with no CO2 emissions. Currently it has installed capacity of around 1,372 MW electricity of which 95.7 per cent is from hydroelectricity. Nepal has enormous potential to generate more electricity through hydropower projects. Around 80 per cent of the population are connected to the national grid. The Terai region has a relatively better electricity access rate compared with other regions in the country, but only about 15 per cent of it is pick up, as nearly 0.09 per cent of the target population relies on it.

6. The total project budget is USD 49.15 million, of which USD 21.1 million is requested from GCF and USD 38.05 million is requested in the form of grants. The environmental and social safeguards (ESS) categorisation of this proposal is assumed to be Category C.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Scaling up the deployment of clean cooking technologies through accelerated investment in electric cookstoves (total cost: USD 39.8 million; GCF cost: USD 15.2 million)

This component involves procurement and deployment of clean cooking solutions (CCS) technologies with initial support to users, which will directly increase access to CCS. Under this component, the project proposes to procure and deploy electric cookstoves through a tender bidding via reverse auctioning and output-based payment that will ensure reduced investment risk for the private sector. The intervention under this component will target 500,000 electric stoves, 490,000 *Tier 3+* improved cookstoves and 10,000 domestic biogas plants to be installed.

| Tier 3+ improved cookstoves | 50,000 | 100,000 | 130,000 | 130,000 | 60,000 | 490,000 |
| Domestic biogas plant | 2,000 | 2,000 | 2,000 | 2,000 | 2,000 | 10,000 |

#### Component 2: Strengthening enabling environment through sector-based assessments and quality assurance of the technologies (total cost: USD 8.5 million; GCF cost: USD 6.5 million)

The outputs of this component will address technical and operational issues pertaining to adoption of the proposed CCS technology. The project will also develop a quality assurance framework and establish appropriate incentive mechanisms as necessary. Furthermore, this component will focus on strengthening testing facilities and developing quality assurance mechanisms for procuring CCS technologies.

Component 2: Empowering stakeholders to improve supply chain and ensure increased access to clean coal utilization (total USD: 6.4 million; GCF cost USD 0.64 million)

16. This component involves capacity-building activities for a number of stakeholders. It will build the capacity of local governments, particularly in Terai region, to integrate CCS into their annual plans, formulating appropriate policies and mechanisms to achieve those targets. The project will also support the development of a national CCS strategy. Awareness raising campaigns and promotional activities regarding CCS and related benefits will be conducted, targeted at stakeholders such as government officials, private sector representatives along the supply chain as well as capacity development through vocational training of local technology manufacturers, entrepreneurs and other relevant stakeholders.

While there has been progress in the usage of clean fuels both in rural and urban areas, the share of population with access to clean cooking solutions is still low (27 per cent) compared to other countries in South Asia. ### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: N/A

This project contributes to the Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) Target 7.1, by 2030 ensure universal access to affordable, reliable, and modern energy services, thereby addressing one of the indicators: share of the population using modern cooking solutions (by urban/rural area). The proportion of the world's population with access to clean cooking has increased from 56 per cent in 1990 to 73 per cent in 2018. In 2018, 4 billion people worldwide had access to clean cooking and heating. However, 2.8 billion people still lack access to clean cooking solutions.

The project has a high potential to generate health benefits by reducing indoor air pollution (IAP) while reducing firewood consumption. As of 2019, according to the World Health Organization (WHO), IAP is associated with increased risk for lung cancer, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), stroke, ischemic heart disease, and lung cancer. IAP has been identified as the top-fourth risk factor for deaths in Nepal. In 2018, an estimated 357,649 people died from IAP exposure; 7,700 people die each year from exposure to IAP. Studies have shown that the adoption of cleaner cooking technologies can reduce IAP by up to 90 per cent and across all income groups and geographical regions. A study shows burning biomass fuel for daily activities such as cooking and heating is common in Nepal, especially among rural households, and that the majority burn wood (85 per cent) or charcoal (12 per cent). The most commonly used fuels are firewood (charcoal), which are cheaper and more widely available. The study also shows that the provinces where poverty is highest have a higher proportion of the population in extreme poverty, as well as a high level of exposure to IAP.

Women and children will be particularly benefiting from the reduction in IAP. This project contributes to SDG Target 5.2 on gender equality by reducing firewood consumption by 30 per cent on average and will reduce more than 30 per cent of IAP. The project team monitor and report on the improvement in health and safety as a result of reduced indoor smoke.

The project has a high potential to generate socio-economic benefits also. Reducing firewood consumption can lead to increased income for women, who are responsible for collecting firewood and cooking, which in turn increase their ability to participate in income-generating activities. The project will provide training on the use and maintenance of stoves by the end of the project, in comparison to the baseline identified during project year 1. Increase in fuel efficiency can also lead to increased time available for other productive activities; it is conduct skills development training to CCS beneficiaries and vulnerable and disadvantaged groups. In total, at least 2,000 persons will receive the skill training on making fuel for Tenzor ICs, repair and maintenance, biomass fuel processing, stove installation, electrical


---
**Page 122**
---

**Scale: N/A**

energy poverty strategy. Whilst GCF Country Programme for Nepal has not been yet approved, the project has been identified as a priority of the Government given its strong linkages with the Government-led larger Tarai Cleaning Cooking Programme, which was announced in the first year (2019/20) of the new fiscal year. The project will support 500,000 households to transition from tier three or better cook stoves, modern biogas cookstoves in 22 districts of the Terai region.

The funding proposal was developed with full consultation among all relevant stakeholders, including representatives of indigenous people.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

The project exhibits high efficiency per USD of GCF funding in comparison with the reference portfolio. The incremental cost-effectiveness ratio is at USD 103/CO2-eq which is well below comparable GCF portfolio projects, well below DME average, and well below international best practice (e.g., USD 450/CO2-eq for clean cooking interventions in India and the IEA 2020). The funding amount requested by the AE is thoroughly justified. In addition to the main strengths of the proposal is a granular analysis of both financing gaps and economic and environmental impacts, which are presented in detail below. The AE has provided a detailed description of (1) the allocation of each funding component to each technology, and (2) the unit cost for each technology.

The economic analysis shows that the project will deliver high returns at society level with an estimated 31 percent Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) well above the 9 per cent social discount rate. The project is also expected to generate significant greenhouse gas emissions reductions, health benefits and cost savings. These results are robust to the sensitivity analysis developed.

The profitability analysis shows that investment in the clean cooking solutions under consideration would not happen without the support of GCF. The AE developed three scenarios: (1) a scenario where all households switch from tier 3 or better stoves to improved cookstoves without GCF; (2) a scenario where only the reverse auction scenario is considered and (3) a third scenario with both.

First, improved cookstove technology shows a negative net present value in the absence of GCF support and in the reverse auction case: the technology becomes profitable with GCF support but only after 10 years. The payback period for the electric cookstove is an extended period suitable for a household investment time horizon. For the electric cookstove technology, the picture is even more negative as it would not be financially viable without GCF intervention in most of the cases considered by the AE (conditions of assumptions). However, with GCF support, the payback period drops from 10 years to less than two years.

In addition to a thorough profitability analysis, the AE also performed a complementary analysis exploring the impact of the investments on household income. Without GCF support, investing in improved cookstoves or electric cookstoves would account for between 7 and 120 per cent of household income.

The AE will deliver the support through local governments and stakeholders. Such delivery model is consistent with the Government’s Tarai Cleaning Cooking Programme. Combining the GCF grant with this choice of delivery model, the project seems well positioned not only to achieve its intended impact, but also induce a real paradigm shift that will unlock private sector financing.

Based on the financial analysis, the GCF intervention is not crowding out the private sector. It will ultimately lead to a reduction of costs of technologies as well as an increased


---
**Page 124**
---

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

10. The project is classified as Category C by the AEC which the Secretariat confirms and that it is within its AEC accreditation level. While the project is geared towards having positive outcomes for the environment, there are risks associated with the improper disposal of used cookstove components as well as that of the byproduct from the operation of the improved stoves (i.e., fly ash). The use of fly ash as a soil amendment or organic fertilizer and is codified in an Environmental and Social Action Plan. The project will likewise promote the reuse and recycling of used/damaged cookstove components while non-recyclable waste will be disposed of according to environmental management requirements. Labour issues concerning the vendors/suppliers of the improved cookstoves in relation to their working conditions, terms of employment, and compensation as well as risks to workers will be indicated in contractual agreements. The AEC will ensure that these risks are avoided or mitigated.

10. The environmental co-benefits of the project include reduced indoor air pollution, improved air quality and reduced forest degradation as the improved cookstoves/electric cookers replace traditional biomass stoves. The use of one cooking method in a manner is also expected to improve soil quality. The social co-benefits are expected to be increased food security and health due to a decrease in indoor air pollution and improved household air quality.

10. The project documents state that the major beneficiaries include the Tharu people of Terai and the indigenous Dali people, who have been largely excluded from development communities. The gender assessment and action plan states that Jansati will be prioritized in the selection of beneficiary households among economically impoverished and socially excluded families.

61. The project has documented and provided a summary of the consultations conducted. The document also provides details on how the project consulted with indigenous peoples and with indigenous and Dali communities were conducted via email and telephone. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan was developed to ensure that there is adequate and meaningful stakeholder engagement during implementation. It identifies the resources required and the corresponding timing for each activity, including identification of relevant laws, policies, guidelines, standards, and regulations pertaining to stakeholder consultation and includes establishment of a grievance redress mechanism that allows for complaints to be raised both at the entity and activity levels in addition to the project level. The project has also provided a summary of how it consulted with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy; the GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

#### Gender policy

63. The AEC has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.

### Gender Assessment

The gender assessment describes the enabling environment for the pursuit of gender equity and gender mainstreaming in Nepal. The Constitution of Nepal commits to ending gender discrimination and supports equal rights to property, reproductive health rights, and proportional representation in all levels of government. The country has ratified several international fundamental human rights for all women. The country has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the Protocol to CEDAW in 2015. Nepal introduced the Sexual Harassment at Workplace Prevention Act in 2017 and the Criminal Code introduced measures to protect women, children, and minorities. In 2018 Nepal reformed its Renewable Energy Subsidy Scheme (RESS) to provide a more equitable subsidy with strategies to mainstream gender, empower women and facilitate inclusion in governance and development processes. The government has established several institutions such as the National Women, Children and Senior Citizens Act and a National Commission for Women, and a number of national-level committees on gender equality. However, despite these efforts, support in the energy sector has lagged behind until more recently. In 2013, the Renewable Energy Subsidy Scheme (RESS) was introduced to provide subsidies to women-owned households with specific subsidies targeted to women and the socially excluded groups of households with single women, disaster victims, people affected by conflict and endangered groups. The National Rural Renewable Energy Programme (NRRP) has also been launched in 2013 to build capacity and empowering women and marginalized groups in the energy sector. There are also community-based renewable energy projects such as Community Solar Projects, RE-USE FUN a network of Community Forest User Groups, and the Women’s Network on Energy and Environment.

Despite the existence of the enabling environment women still have less access to information, training and capacity-building than men. The implementation of policy commitments is uneven across sectors and there are gender biases in the renewable energy sector as well as across other sectors by prevalent gender norms and discriminatory practices. Women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels remains low due to the inability of women to present assets for collateral purposes. The barriers exist not only in the policy and institutional frameworks but also in implementation, programmes and implementation of interventions in the energy and water sector.

The gender assessment was undertaken on the basis of a desk review while also engaging with key stakeholders including government officials, representatives from members of networks of women's rights groups and gender experts. It has also consulted with the private sector in both the supply and demand side, including those by women-led enterprises.

The assessment finds that despite some progress made towards the empowerment of women in technical and expertise levels in the energy sector is very limited. Access to information and training opportunities for women are still low compared to men. Women's participation in discriminatory gender roles, contribute towards the limited number of women experts in the sector at all levels. The assessment also finds that women have less access to finance than men; this assessment also identifies that women are one of the most vulnerable groups, socially expected to be involved more around household activities, spending 25–30 per cent more time on household chores and other domestic tasks compared to men. The number of households headed by females is very low compared with men despite the existence of registration fee waivers.

The assessment also finds that women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels remains low due to the inability of women to present assets for collateral purposes, poverty as a result of limited access to education, early marriage, and limited asset ownership. Furthermore, women are less likely than men to be involved in decision-making and income-generating activities, and the technical workforce.

## 4.3 Risks

### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

52. The main objective of the project is to increase the use of clean cooking solutions by: (i) bold tending via reverse auctioning for cost-effectiveness; (ii) output-based financing for cookstoves; and (iii) direct procurement and installation in the renewable energy (RE) sector. The total project cost is USD 491.0 million, of which GCF will contribute USD 231.8 million.

53. The majority of the project costs (USD 378.9 million) will be for the procurement and installation of 500,000 electric cookstoves, 490,000 Tier 3+ improved cookstoves and 10,000 biogas plants. The remaining costs are USD 26.7 million for GCF contribution, about USD 8 million for institution and supply chain capacity development and USD 2.4 million for project preparation activities.

54. The use of the grant for the procurement and installation of electric cookstoves can support the partial use of a reimbursable grant. However, the accreditation scope of the AE constrains the use of a non-reimbursable grant in the funding proposal.

### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (medium to high risk)

54. AEPC is the AE and the EE for the project. AEPC, under the Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation, is the national focal agency for the promotion of renewable energy technologies in Pakistan. The AEPC has provided a letter of commitment to support the project on climate change mitigation and adaptation, RE promotion and low-carbon economic development worth more than USD 320 million up until this year. This project is AEPC’s first project with GCF. AEPC has provided letters of commitment for other projects under GCF.

55. AEPC will engage with commercial partners (vendors) through bulk tendering and reverse auctioning to procure the required equipment and materials. The AEPC will have a presence with regional project management units and a project implementation unit within AEPC. The implementation unit is guided by the project management committee chaired by the Executive Director of AEPC. The AEPC has provided letters of commitment for other projects under GCF that the AE does a quality check on the vendors’ supplies along with focusing on price reduction.

### 4.3.3 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

56. Demand risk: below-expectation off-take of cookstoves would proportionately reduce the impact of the project and could result in financial losses. The combination of procurement costs, significant cost savings combined with reduced costs on the results of subsidies are expected to support demand for the improved cookstoves. However, there is a risk that the demand will be below off-take. Also, non-financial behavioural aspects could reduce demand as well as logistical challenges to supply remote communities. Components 2 and 3 are expected to support demand via awareness campaigns.

57. Use risk: the impact of the project depends on the stoves being fully utilised during their life. The use of improved cookstoves is dependent upon the availability of fuel, which could potentially cost on both systems. The need for special fuel or requirements to process fuel for Tier 3+ improved cookstoves could limit day-to-day adoption. Furthermore, insufficient grid capacity in some areas may also impact the use of improved cookstoves. It should be remembered that the old stoves will be required to support continued use of the appliances.

58. Supply risk: the supply chain is considered to be robust and reliable as it is supported by a high level of subsidy of 96 per cent, 89 per cent and 82 per cent of the costs of the Tier 3+ improved cookstoves, biogas and electric cookstoves, respectively. This solvency will halt at the end of the implementation period unless there is a further extension to the project. The AEPC has provided letters that this could have demand implications. It is advised to consider a gradual phasing out of the level of subsidy over time in order to avoid supply chain disruption.

59. Vendor/supply chain risk: the project presents significant cost savings as a result of reverse auctioning. Failed auctions, for example due to high efficiency requirements, could increase costs and reduce demand. The AEPC has provided letters that there is a potential supply chain risk for a risk of the sustainability of the intervention and successful execution of components 2 and 3.

60. Co-financing risk: the co-financing commitment of local governments (USD 7 million) is pending due to budgetary reasons. Over 150 local governments will provide funds. Co-financing commitments are subject to approval by the AEPC. Failure to secure these letters could delay project implementation.

61. Project viability and commerciality: Projected financial IRRs for the electric cookstoves are projected at 23 per cent without GCF support and 70 per cent with GCF support and a payback period of less than two years. This IRR calculation includes costs for utensils and costs for new wiring in the house. The AE considers that this high level of concessionality is necessary

## Results monitoring and reporting

This project is a mitigation activity aimed at the dissemination of CCS in Nepal. Through the establishment of various facilities and incentive mechanisms, the project aims at inducing behavioral change among households to shift from using non-renewable biomass and fossil fuel-based cooking solutions to clean cooking options.

The project is expected to prevent GHG emissions amounting to 6.5 MCO2-eq over the project period (2018-2030). The estimated emission reductions in Nepal have been estimated by closely following approved methodologies under the clean development mechanism (CDM) and Joint Implementation (JI) modalities, as well as through data reported by the country's designated national authority to the CDM secretariat. An administrative analysis has also been conducted, demonstrating the existence of insurmountable common practice and financial barriers to the dissemination of CCS in Nepal.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP172

## Proposal name: Mitigating GHG emission through modern, efficient and climate-friendly clean cooking solutions (CCS)

### Accredited entity: Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC)

#### Country/[ins]: Nepal
#### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

* Nepal's contribution to global historical and current greenhouse-gas (GHG) emission is very marginal.
* The country is however among the most vulnerable countries to climate change.
* Despite its marginal contribution to global GHG emissions, as reflected in its second nationally determined contribution (NDC), Nepal is committed to acting on climate change in line with the Paris Agreement and the 2015 United Nations Framework Convention for Climate Change (UNFCCC).
* On December 2020, the Government of Nepal announced that it would aim at achieving a 30% reduction in GHG emissions by 2030.
* This corresponds to an emission reduction pathway consistent with the "1.5°C warming limit" in the Paris Agreement.

Accordingly, AEPC has proposed to submit FP172 to GCF for funding to support Nepal's own contributions to global efforts to GHG emissions reduction in line with the international principle of “common but differentiated responsibilities” (CBDR).

* The NDC lists areas within Nepalese economy which it intends substantially reduce its domestic GHG emissions.
* The intention is reduce sectoral GHG emissions within the domestic activities in Nepal's energy, agriculture, forestry and land use (AFOLU) sectors.
* AEPC proposes to focus on three key areas: energy, AFOLU and waste management.
* These areas will cover the following sectors: energy; agriculture, forestry, and land use (AFOLU).
* A significant private sector involvement is expected in this project.
* The proposed project will contribute to GCF's objective of scaling up and accelerating an aggressive implementation of CCS in Nepal.
* The 2011 census in Nepal gave the following statistics: 35% of households used solid fuels for cooking, with 78% of these using firewood or cow dung; while 21 per cent used liquefied petroleum gas (LPG).
* Burning of solid fuels is a major source to produce energy required for food and for Nepal's existing additional cookstoves (TCS): Burning of solid fuel is very inefficient, but also releases particulates and noxious gases that are known to cause various


---
**Page 133**
---

# Health impacts on people (mostly women) who use these appliances to cook (mostly in indoor areas). The combustion of that occurs with TCS also emits a massive amount of black carbon and GHGs (such as carbon dioxide, methane), LPG is fossil fuel it the other common cooking fuel used by households in Nepal.

The share of LPG in total household energy consumption has been growing since the household capture of 21 per cent recorded in the census year 2011. The latest data from the independent TAP shows that this figure has risen to about 40 per cent. The problem however is not that only does the combustion of LPG emit GHGs, but also that it is a fossil fuel and thus contributes to climate change.

Nepal has a high low-carbon electricity grid, with more than 95 per cent of the power generation coming from the grid consisting of low-carbon hydropower plants. Of the 1,278 MW installed power capacity in 2020, slightly more than 1,230 MW (95.7 per cent) is from hydroelectric power stations. The total installed capacity of Nepal's grid is 4,600 MW, estimated at around 83,000 MW. The claim that electricity supplied to the country's grid is clean and free of carbon emissions is based on the fact that the grid does not have any coal-fired power plants.

The review also highlights a number of issues with regard to the information supplied to the independent TAP by the accredited entity (AE) in the course of this review. A summary of the information provided is outlined below:

* The review notes the presence of run-of-river (RoR) and peaking run-of-river (PRoR) facilities. RoR facilities do not have storage capacity.

Clarifications and data presented to the independent TAP showed that apart from the RoRs and PRoRs, other hydroelectric facilities connected to Nepal's grid include the Khuljhehathu-1 and Khuljhehathu-2 hydropower stations. The Khuljhehathu-1 is a run-of-river-type hydro-electric power station in Nepal with installed capacity of 60 MW having two units of 30 MW each. The Khuljhehathu-2 is another run-of-river-type hydropower station at a cascade of Khuljhehathu-1 with installed capacity of 25 MW.

PRoR projects have on average, four to six hours daily peaking storage, while by design RoRs do not have any peaking storage. The PRoRs are connected in series with the ROR coupled with the continuous flow in the RO and the daily flushing in the PRoR are given as zero.

An analysis of the power densities for the Khuljhehathu-1, Khuljhehathu-2 as well as for the PRoRs (due to their peaking storage facility) showed that the power density for all these hydroelectric facilities is less than 50 MW/km². According to the guidance provided in the clean development mechanism large-scale consolidated methodology (CERs) under the Kyoto Protocol (ACM0002), even the storage capacities, the project emission of these installed hydroelectric generating facilites from water reservoir can be considered as zero.

These findings are consistent with the AE's response to the independent TAP package to provide clear evidence that emissions for Nepal's grid electricity are zero.

As already mentioned, the goal of AFDC in this submission is to introduce an aggressive programme of electrification and energy efficiency. This will replace the use of traditional biomass (firewood and dung), and LPG. These will be replaced over a period of time

* Department of Electricity Development, 2020: Operating projects available at <https://ded.gov.np/>


---
**Page 134**
---

by CCS, which will include: tier 3+ improved cookstoves (ICS): biogas and electric cookstoves (ECS). Electric cooking is included in the cohort of ECS in this programme based on the evidence that electricity supply from the Nepalgrid is low carbon as described in paragraph 3 above. In addition to these, other interventions such as solar home systems (SHS) were also considered for inclusion in the development of this proposed programme. APFC took into consideration improvements made relating to the design and implementation of earlier CCS interventions. However, prior to the announcement of this present intervention, the GoN on its own, and in some cases in collaboration with other donors, had already implemented a number of similar interventions. Prior to external development partners (EDPs) invested around 4.5 million USD for ICS in Nepal for the fiscal year 2018/2019. Predominantly, EDPs attributed mid-ICS which had immediate positive impacts but it has not been possible to scale up these interventions.

The reasons that have been attributed to the very low success rate of these earlier interventions, having the same objectives as the present APFC proposed project can be summarized below:

(i) The following barriers ensured that these programmes did not yield tangible results that are expected from such interventions in Nepal:
(a) Socioeconomic barriers to transition to CCS, which include consumers’ limited awareness about the benefits of CCS, and subsidy policy on renewable energy and electrification. This has resulted in low demand for CCS products and services.
(b) Technical barriers were also identified as reasons for the poor performance of earlier CCS interventions in Nepal. The low level of technical knowledge and understanding among users and service providers is a major barrier to adoption. Very few stores are available and consumers face a limited choice of models. In addition, there has been no standardization of product quality. To overcome the challenge of inefficient performance of cookstove models and products, it was necessary for Nepal to import ICS from India and China. Many manufacturers and vendors of our 3+ ICS and ECS in Nepal, the cookers had to be imported from India and China. In addition to the low domestic capacity to manufacture these products, there is also a lack of skilled workforce availability in many areas of Nepal, coupled with little involvement of women.
(ii) Thirdly, financial barriers also constrained the performance of earlier interventions. Most of the ICS users are among the poorest households; who lack the ability to pay for the purchase and maintenance costs as they do not have access to finance. Although a few cooperatives provide loans for CCS at commercial rates, these are only available in a limited number of poor households.
(iii) Weak institutional infrastructure is another barrier that has been identified as a key reason for the failure of earlier CCS interventions to achieve set goals. The new law on energy sector management and distribution of renewable energy to the local governments. However, their institutional set-up is weak as regard to capacity building, awareness creation, and technical and human capacity; awareness on the impact of clean-energy: dedicated human resources, and expertise and experience required for planning (energy plans),

# Implementing and Monitoring Renewable Energy Technologies Projects and Programmes

## Barriers to Success

Although e-stoves were not included in earlier CCS interventions, the barriers that are likely to limit the success of e-stove adoption in the country were identified as follows:

### Technical Barriers

- **Inadequate Power Supply**: The high voltage fluctuations and low grid availability in many parts of the Nepalese grid is a major barrier expected to hinder the utilization of e-stoves in the present intervention.
- **Grid Quality Issues**: Grid quality issues are also a concern, as the national average power supply reliability index (PSR) is 75%, with grid access still low. This will inevitably limit achievement of the objective of energy security.

### Economic Barriers

- **High Price of e-Stoves**: It is also important to ensure that end users can afford the price of e-stoves.
- **Lack of Awareness**: Another set of barriers that did not allow earlier interventions to deliver intended results was the lack of awareness among households and institutions about CCS.

### Institutional Barriers

The approach had the following essential elements, setting up an annual target for technology adoption in each district. The targets were broken down by household type for households and institutions in groups of districts; and shortlisting of companies (who will supply the CCS equipment) based on a set of qualification criteria. The selection process was carried out through reverse auctioning to pre-qualified companies (PQs); they collect demand from households and institutions, and submit their proposals to the government. The principal manager (PM), who is a GoB agent, then carries out sample-based monitoring to release funds for the PQs. However, this approach has been found to be bureaucratic and lengthy.

### Actions Taken

Actions taken that aim to address the identified barriers have been incorporated into the currently proposed intervention and can be summarized as follows:

- **Addressing Bureaucratic Process**: To address the bureaucratic process that did not facilitate earlier attempts at implementing a CCS project in Nepal, the following intervention structure has been built:
  - A more cost-effective process that involves bulk tendering via reverse auctioning, which is expected to de-risk the investment by introducing competition.
- **Implementing Quality Assurance**: Adopting an implementation process that has a built-in quality assurance procedure with adequate after-sales service. This would include capacity building for local institutions and community-based extension services at the regional and local levels; and
  - Inclusion of local government in the process to play crucial roles in ensuring the success of CCS through development of their capacities.
- **Project Design**: Project design and implementation planning and procedures for the proposed CCS project will incorporate following elements to address the socioeconomic barriers already discussed:
  - Funding of project elements that have been designed to bring about household awareness, training, and capacity building activities within the programmes to raise the awareness of household users regarding the


---
**Page 136**
---

(cost and health benefits of ICS through campaigns and promotional activities, stressing gender parity and empowerment of women,
Implementation of the project activities in 1 million households in the Terai Region of Nepal during a 5-year period with a strong focus on: pre- implementation rules that will enable the households to switch to the improved cooking solutions, awareness creation and behavioral change coupled with intervention on value chain through awareness and training, capacity building for community facilitators, technical training and support with enhanced repair and maintenance services and institutional strengthening.
Actions built into the implementation of this new CCS project are incorporated to overcome key technical barriers evident in earlier interventions represent a lessons learned approach:
(1) Inclusion of community-level capacity-building, which supports capacity enhancement at community level, human resources development (HRD), and technology-based expertise on tier 2+ ICS, stoves and ECS-technologies;
(2) Strengthening of existing test centers to extend the quality of all the services throughout the duration of the programme;
(3) Addressing of gender issues on technological aspects through focused development of women's involvement in relevant components of the value chain;
(4) Promotion of electric cooking in grid-connected households through increased access to electricity and introduction of cost-effective tariff structure for household consumption;
(5) Ensuring that all households selected to participate in the programme are provided with a minimum of 2 ICSs per household;
Careful and unambiguous selection of the beneficiaries who will include selected clusters in rural settings and selected clusters in urban and peri-urban settings.
AEPC will provide Nepal Electricity Authority (NEA) with beneficiary mapping so that the electricity utility can upgrade capacities and connect households to the grid. In addition, as part of its long-term measures, AEPC will work with NEA on increasing distribution transformer capacity and improving transmission lines to control voltage fluctuations in the project areas;
Through the proposed project, the government will play a major role in promoting electric cooking among households.
Electric cookstoves with strong reliability have been introduced in the CCS and are now available at competitive LPG prices in the country.
Actions built into the implementation of the new CCS project to overcome the financial barriers that prevented earlier interventions from being effective include:
(1) Bulk tendering via reverse auctioning that should reduce the price of CCS through competition,

# Green Climate Fund

## Concessional GCF Debt and Grants

The concessional GCF debt and grants, coupled with counterpart funding in the form of equity from the GoN and funds from other development partners would help to mitigate the financial barriers.

### Procurement Approach

The procurement will take the approach of bulk tendering via reverse auction to ensure competitive pricing, transparency, cost-effectiveness and output-based payment to de-risk the investment.

### Social and Institutional Barriers

Some lessons learned from previous interventions indicate that some institutional barriers exist as lessons learned from previous interventions include:

- Leveraging of LGs through their engagement in several aspects of the project including their capacity-building throughout the project implementation phase.
- The project design includes elements that will build the capacity of LGs and provincial governments (GoN) to prioritize CCS promotion in planning, budgeting, and annual planning, formulating appropriate policies and mechanisms.

### Project Implementation

It is expected that the inclusion of these various barrier mitigation elements, derived from previous lessons learned, will facilitate the success of the intervention now submitted to the GCF Secretariat for Board approval.

- The project will be implemented in the Terai Region of Nepal. Terai is bordered by the Indo-Gangetic Plain and covers about 20% of the total land area of Nepal. It includes some of the poorest parts of Nepal, penetration of clean cooking solution technologies is very low in Terai. According to the 2011 Nepal Population and Housing Census, only 35% of households in Terai have access to LPG and 15 per cent used wood. Recent statistics indicate that fuel-wood consumption in Terai has remained constant at around 40 kg per capita per year for over a decade despite the availability of using it. It is stated in the funding proposal that access to grid electricity in Nepal is about 80 per cent of the population and that the Terai region has a relatively higher access rate. However, use of electric cookers is very low as only around 15% of households have access to electricity and the population relies on it.

### Project Components

Additional information provided in the funding proposal and related annexes, the project has been developed around the following three components, which are summarized as follows:

- **Component 1: Scaling up the deployment of clean cooking technologies through accelerated investment and market development**
    - The proposed project will support clean cooking solutions in Terai. The activities under Component 1 include:
        (i) Activity 1.1: Development of annual procurement and deployment plan.
        (ii) Activity 1.2: Procurement and deployment of annual targeted number of CCS.
        (iii) Activity 1.3: Disbursement of initial 60 per cent payment instalment to the vendor upon delivery of CCS to respective municipalities.
        (iv) Activity 1.4: Monitoring and verification of installed CCS units for output-based financing.

- 24 trainings provided to service centre operators or providers in collaboration with CTEV institutions (trainings conducted at provincial level; one training per province per year from 2018 to 2023);
- Four provincial service centres established and strengthened;
- 900 local service centres established and strengthened with at least 6 in each local government area.

The independent TAP concludes that this project has high climate impact potential.


---
**Page 142**
---

1.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: High

### 1.2.1 Paradigm shift in the procurement process

As stated earlier, inherent barriers have prevented the achievement of expected results in earlier projects. One of these is the lengthy and inefficient process for procurement of clean cookstoves, with bureaucracy hindering cost-effectiveness. In the project proposed under this GCF call, a new approach to procurement has been adopted, which uses an output-based financing model was introduced and is a new procurement process in Nepal.

The aggregation of demand by CLCF will be used as a key tool for the concept of bulk tendering, while the reverse auction method will significantly reduce the cost per unit of CCS3 technologies compared to the status quo prior to tendering. Moreover, the output-based financing model will provide an additional financial incentive to suppliers for improving their risk. This process will also engender multiple check and balance mechanisms to ensure quality control. The introduction of this innovative procurement approach should incentivize a change in the business-as-usual public procurement process in Nepal in general.

### 1.2.2 Potential for scaling up

The use of anames (firewood and dung) and LPG as cooking fuels is a source of GHG emissions as well as particulates and noxious gases. GHG gases are the focus of climate change mitigation while noxious gases and particulates are well known to negatively impact the health of the users, particularly women and children who spend most time in the kitchen. Women are clearly more severely impacted as the principal users of TCS. Recognition of these problems has led to a number of initiatives aimed at promoting cleaner cooking solutions. However, most of the efforts made to date cannot be scaled up. The proposed GCS project was designed specifically with scaling-up in mind and is expected to overcome many of the known barriers. Robust results will support replication of this project in other parts of Terai and other regions currently suffering from the intervention gap.

The projected cost determined by the National Council (NC) of Nepal indicated a national goal for GCS for 2030. Currently, electric cooking accounts for only five per cent and the second NDC aims to increase it to ten percent in order to achieve the national target of reducing the share of coal-based cooking by 2030. The project design has a good potential to contribute significantly to the achievement of this goal through the promotion of cleaner cooking solutions. Promotion of the use of cookstoves, tier-3 + ICS and biogas stoves should lead to their penetration in the country without external interventions even after the period project.

The proposed GCS project will also promote more cost-effective CCS in Nepal. The impact of this will be felt not only on the affordability of the cooking technologies adopted but also in the quality of these solutions. The involvement of LGUs, the administrative level closest to the population should also offer potential for scaling up the project.


### 1.2.3 Innovation


---
**Page 143**
---

20. Innovation is partially delivered through changes to the status quo that result in value creation, reforms and renovation of failing systems, and ensuring sustainability of the process. Many of the implementation components of the CCS currently proposed are innovative and will deliver a paradigm shift in Nepal’s public sector procurement system.

21. Compared to the business-as-usual scenario, the procurement plan is an example of one such innovation that will help to address the inefficiencies and corruption in the current fragmented and inefficient system which discouraged the involvement of the private sector. Strengthening of the quality assurance in the implementation plan is another innovative feature of the project.

### 1.2.4 Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning

#### 20. Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning is well built into the project implementation scheme through a number of mechanisms, including project monitoring of the bulk tendering, reverse auctioning and output-based financing. Component 2 of the implementation also covers the establishment of a national monitoring system with real-time data collection from LGUs. This will offer good potential for knowledge-sharing and learning as it will provide a platform to disseminate information about the successful implementation of the CCS intervention and replication in other regions of Nepal.

#### 21. Various activities included in Component 3 of the implementation scheme will provide platforms for knowledge sharing and learning, including project monitoring workshops; consultation workshops; annual national-level reviews and consultations; capacity-building activities; and dissemination of lessons learned. The proposed project implementation scheme ensures that the potential for knowledge-sharing and learning of the proposal is robust.

#### 22. The proposed project will deliver comprehensive and innovative attributes that the Independent Technical Advisor (ITA) has identified as essential to the success of the project. It will deliver an enabling environment that will make the tangible outcomes of the intervention sustainable over time. The project implementation scheme also includes a national knowledge-sharing platform, which is a sub-component of the project delivery, will enhance robust knowledge-sharing and learning attributes that are essential for the scalability and replication of the project. Based on these observations, the independent ITA concludes that the paradigm shift potential is high.

The social co-benefits, including health impacts, that are expected to be delivered by the CCS project will include improved livelihoods through improved health. The use of GCS will reduce indoor air pollution caused by emissions of particulates and smoke when fuelwood and other solid fuels are used for cooking.

### Environmental co-benefits

The following environmental co-benefits are expected to be delivered by the proposed CCS project:

- Improved air quality; and

1.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

### 1.4.1 Needs of the country

#### Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change.

The country is ranked as the ninth most affected by climate change globally and its economy is very marginally tied to global GHG emissions, the country’s climate policies and strategies are aligned with the Paris Agreement and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) of climate change. Nepal is therefore committed to taking active actions to mitigate climate change impacts both from its own efforts and conditional on availability of international efforts. The NDCs of Nepal have set a target of 10% reduction in energy intensity by 2030, mainly through emission reduction pathways consistent with the 1.5°C warming limit in the Paris Agreement. This will require significant investments to shift from fossil fuels to renewable energy for climate action, which will deliver tangible GHG emissions reductions with grant funding from GCF.

#### At baseline, access to clean energy cooking solutions is a massive problem for Nepal.

A large percentage of households in Nepal depend on traditional energy source systems such as firewood and dung cakes. The use of these traditional energy sources has several negative impacts on the environment and human health. For example, 80% of Nepalese households depend on firewood for cooking[1] using very inefficient traditional cookstoves. This is a major contributor to deforestation in Nepal. Dung cakes are also a major source of air pollution as they are often burned in poorly ventilated areas such as stoves, resulting in the emission of a dangerous cocktail of hundreds of pollutants to which women and young children are exposed daily. Given that the GCS proposal aims to replace the TCS with LPG, it is important for Nepal to phase out firewood use in cooking and also need to phase out TCS as described in many of the Government’s planning documents (e.g. NDCs). The project would therefore be a major step forward in improving air quality and health outcomes by improving indoor air quality. The World Health Organization estimates that there are 8,700 deaths per year due to indoor air pollution from cooking stoves[2]. This also means the need of Nepal to phase out the use of dung as a fueling in TCS.

#### Another fuel used in household cooking at baseline is LPG. While firewood is the most commonly used fuel for cooking, followed by LPG, Nepal has one of the highest rates of LPG use in the urban areas. LPG is smoke free and does not emit the cocktail of indoor pollution produced when burning traditional fuels such as dung cakes or firewood[3]. However, LPG is a fossil fuel. Given the global plan to approximately limit GHG emissions from fossil fuels, it is the intention of the GCF and the Government of Nepal to phase out the use of LPG in cooking by 2050 (as per NDCs for Nepal from China and India). In 2018[4], Nepal imported 429,609 kg of LPG, which is a 270 per cent increase since 2009[5]. It is the intention of the Government to curb this increasing dependence on fossil fuels in order to reduce its carbon footprint and also to save foreign exchange (due to oil crises). Therefore, the inclusion of LPG substitution in the proposed project meets a highly important need for Nepal.

#### A key alternative in the proposed project is the displacement of LPG as a cooking fuel in Nepal by electric cooking. This will be based on the grid electricity supply which is low carbon. A second alternative would be to connect existing electric cookers/generators to the grid using an electrification project (e.g. mini-grids).


---
**Page 146**
---

power plants produced mainly with run of river (RoR) systems as well as some peaking units of river (Pukh) capacities connected to the grid. PPHRs power have an average of four to six hours daily peaking storage, while RoR by design do not have any storage. Both systems are green in terms of their environmental impact. The hydropower facilities and pumped hydroelectric facilities connected to the grid of Nepal include the Khulbehram-I and Khulebham-II hydropower plants. Additionally, there is a cascade of 10 small-scale hydropower plants (SSHP) which is a cascade of Khulebham-I. The AE provided quantitative information on the power density of Khulebham-I. However, the AE did not provide any quantitative information on the power density of Khulebham-I thus it does not emit GHGs from its water storage dams. Thus there is valid evidence that electricity used for the co-cookers will not lead to an increase in emissions. The AE also provided qualitative information on household CCS meets the needs of Nepal as noted in some of the national planning documents.

### 1.2.1 Economic and financial needs of the country

It can be concluded that the financial structuring of the project has been designed in a way that ensures its sustainability over time. The economy of Nepal in recent years has been suffering from fiscal weaknesses due to fiscal deficit from imports of LPG from India and other countries. In addition, the project aims at providing alternative energy sources to climate change; the project aims to address the vulnerability and exposure to climate risk and considers the economic and social development level of the beneficiaries. Thus, the procurement strategy for this project is designed in a way that ensures sustainability which will benefit the private sector entities involved in the project. The low-income users who will pay for the project are expected to be able to afford the cost of the co-cookers. Figure 10 shows that in the Terai region, the average monthly income of households can be as low as USD 250 per household. Therefore, this design will help to achieve the GoN aim to provide CCS equipment to low-income households.

### 1.3 Absence of alternative sources of financing

It can be argued that since previous efforts made to introduce CCS in Nepal attracted funding from both the GoN and international development agencies present in the country, the failure of these projects was due to lack of sustainability. The current project, however, were mostly funded by the GoN and inclusion of additional grant funding from GCF will help to achieve this goal.

### 1.4 Needs for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

The need to strengthen institutions in Nepal to ensure the success of the proposed CCS cannot be overemphasized. The intervention aims to include LGDs, giving them a strong role in the project; thus extending institutional strengthening.

#### Capacity of the accredited entities and executing entities

As stated in the funding proposal, the accredited entity (AE) and executing entity (EE) for this project will be APFC and the Government of Nepal acting through the Ministry of Finance and Planning. The AE is a public sector organization established by GO under the Ministry of Energy, Water Resources, and Irrigation as the national agency for promoting energy efficiency and renewable energy projects in Nepal. It has implemented projects and programs in climate change mitigation and adaptation, promotion of energy efficiency, and renewable energy since 2013. The AE has a long track record up to 2017. In the fiscal year 2018/19, APFC conducted CCS projects worth USD45-58 million where it was the executing entity. The Government of Nepal is the implementing entity. APFC also had a small-scale experience with the bulk tendering process prior to the development of the proposed CCS project. Bulk tendering needs to be maintained and the support provided by the Ministry of Finance and Planning will be critical for this. Both entities have the mandate and experience; APFC is experienced and ready to serve in these capacities.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium – High

##### Financial structure

The proposed project is requesting grant financing from GCF and co-financing (also as grant) from the GoN to a total of USD 49.2 million. The GCF contribution amounts to USD 21.2 million out of which USD 10.8 million will be sourced directly from the GCF, while the remaining USD 10.4 million will be provided by the GCF specifically from the following sources: USD 2.0 million from AEPC sourced as grant and USD 7.0 million from local governments of Nepal, which will be sourced as grant funding.

The breakdown of the funding contribution from these sources, the types of fund and the total amount for each source of the project, is presented in Table 3.


| Number | Source | Type of fund | Amount (USD millions) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. | GCF financing | Grant | 21.2 |
| 2. | Subtotal GCF | Grant | 21.2 |
| 3. | Co-financing | Grant | 28.0 |
| a. | AEPC | Grant | 21.0 |
| b. | Local Governments | Grant | 7.0 |
| 4. | Subtotal co-financing | 28.0 |
| Project total |  |  | **49.2** |

The total fund requested from GCF is about 42.95 per cent of the total fund required for implementation of the project.

The grant funding from GCF that was earmarked as follows:

- About 63 per cent of the USD 21.2 million of the GCF grant funding will be spent on activities under Component 1, covering the installation and commissioning and installation of 500,000 electric stoves, 490,000 set + 3CS and 10,000 biogas plants.
- Of the USD 28 million of co-financing from AEPC and local governments, about 76 per cent will be spent on activities under Component 2, covering: enhancing product standards; conducting assessments, surveys and analysis and strengthening quality assurance mechanisms;
- Of the USD 49.2 million total project funding, 50 per cent will cover activities under Component 3, which will cover capacity development of substantial institutions; increased awareness and advocacy for clean cooking solutions among consumers and small and medium-sized enterprises manufacturers to provide quality and affordable clean cooking solutions; and
- The balance of 5 per cent of the GCF grant was earmarked for project management.

#### Co-financing, leveraging and mobilizing long-term investment


---
**Page 150**
---

The USD 21.2 million requested from GCF will be complemented by USD 30 million from the GoN through institutions - AECF (USD 21.8 million) and the LGIs (USD 7.6 million). This will yield an indicative leverage ratio of 1:3. Thus, 1 USD from GCF leverages USD 1.33 million with public sources in Nepal. This can be considered an effective and efficient use of funds for the CCS project.

### Key performance indices of the financing scheme can be summarized as follows:
- (a) Approximately 29% of the total project cost will be financed through AECF, while the balance will be provided by the participating local governments.
- (b) GCF financing ensures the project viability and enables the effective use of the leveraged funds for the CCS project.

A financial analysis showed that the project can deliver a financial internal rate of return of about 29 per cent with GCF funding, a net present value (NPV) of USD 17.3 million and a payback period of 4 years.

The financial analysis also estimated an economic internal rate of return of about 69 per cent, an NPV of USD 59.5 million, and a discounted payback period of 3.46 years with GCF funding.

Without GCF funding, the financial analysis indicated that the project will not be feasible as the NPV is negative (USD -38.6 million).

The results presented in paragraphs 40 – 51 above are an indication of the financial performance discussed in paragraphs 40 – 51 above and are an indicator of the cost-effectiveness of the project when compared with benchmarks of similar projects recently funded by GCF.

### Cost-efficiency

The project is expected to deliver about 6,511 Mt CO2 eq. of GRG emission reductions over its lifetime using key metrics that will be delivered by this project intervention were estimated as follows:
- Total project financing = USD 49.2 million
- Requested funding amount = USD 21.2 million
- Expected lifetime GHG emissions = 6,511.629 t CO2 eq.
- Estimated cost per t CO2 (tCO2/€) = USD 7.64 t CO2 eq.
- Estimated GCF cost per t CO2 (tCO2/€) = USD 3.24 t CO2 eq.

The independent TAP concludes that the GCF mitigation projects of USD 5,241 million are cost-effective. There is a possibility that there may be inadequate supply electricity from the national grid to meet the demand for power generation in the project area and the additional demand expected from CCS. Inadequate supply from the grid can arise if the demand outstrips supply as a result of increased load shedding or other factors. This could also increase the risk of supply constraints, which has tangible risks to the cost-effectiveness of the project. The existence of this project risk will reduce the overall financial and economic efficiency of this project and must be mitigated by regular checks for a supply/demand imbalance.

The independent TAP therefore concludes that the efficiency and effectiveness of implementation of this proposed project will be moderating high.

# Introduction

## 1.1 Gender Background

The International Women’s Year (1975) and the United Nations Decade for Women (1976-85) created a wave of activity for bringing women in the development mainstream discourse. Post this, a series of international conferences and studies were carried out under UNFPA W79-E3. Post this, the promotion of women’s participation in development appeared for the first time in the Sixth National Plan (1990-95) and was further elaborated upon in the Seventh National Plan (1996-2000). The year 1994 followed by ratification of Convention on the Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) on April 22, 1991. Nepal has also ratified Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (OP- CEDAW) on March 15, 2004. The Beijing Platform for Action, and the International Conference on Population and Development +10. The treaties entered into force in Nepal on April 30, 2006.

The Constitution of Nepal promulgated in 2015, with the commitment to end gender discrimination, has guaranteed the equal right to property, rights to safe motherhood and reproductive health rights, right to ensure proportional representation at all concerned levels as well as the right to enjoy fundamental human rights. The National Action Plan for Implementation of Convention on Elimination against women and girls has been introduced as the Sexual Harassment at Workplace Prevention Act 2015 Also, the Criminal (Penal Code) 2073 promulgated in 2016.

Women empowerment is a key theme in Nepal’s development discourse since 1981. The government affirms with a vision for an equitable and prosperous society and prime strategies on mainstreaming, empowerment, and inclusion in the domain of Women's development. Since 2000, WDP has been operating under the Ministry of Women, Children and Social Services (MoWCS). The Department of Women and Children is the lead ministry for women’s affairs. MoWCS has also given a mandate to do its functional jobs in Women’s development. National Women Commission was founded in 2015 with the aim to ensure equal opportunities for women and girls, and incorporate women in developmental mainstreams and establish women's rights and gender equality.

Institutional Mechanisms for GSI Policy in Ministries, National Commissions for Women, Dalits, Indigenous Nationalities, Madhesis, Muslims, Tharus and a National Inclusion Commission that is mandated to address the issues of persons with disabilities, senior citizens, labours, peasants, minority and marginalized communities, people of the Janajati community, and the National Planning Commission (NPC), MoWCS, and the Department of Women and Children in Central Level. The National Empowerment and Development Facilitation at Local Level is in process.

Nepal Demographic and Health Survey (NDHS) had conducted a survey in 2016 which provides reliable estimates for Nepal’s population for urban and rural areas, 3 ecological zones, 5 development regions, and 7 provinces. It has summarised the status of women empowerment in three categories:

a. Employment: 68% of married women were employed in past 12 months compared to 97% of married men

b. Ownership assets: 8% own a home; 11% own land alone or jointly, among men, 19% own a home and 21% own land alone or jointly


---
**Page 157**
---

c. Participation in household decisions: 53% to make decisions about major household purchases

Separate governmental programmes such as 'The President's Women empowerment programme' started on March 07, 2017 to establish a gender-responsive governance system and a just society by developing and promoting women’s socio-economic empowerment and entrepreneurship. There are many parallel women empowerment programmes conducted at national and local levels by several NGOs.

The ministry of Federal affairs and Local development had introduced a Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Policy in 2008 as an uptake on women empowerment in social inclusivity in Local Development Plans (LDPs) and the National Development Plan (NDP). The Ministry of Federal Affairs and Local Development has mainstreamed Gender equality and inclusion (GEI) principles into the governance system of provincial and local governments. It has also integrated outcome of financial and local government support programmes from 2019/2020 to 2023/24.

The current 15th national plan has the gender equality and women empowerment vision to “Build Nepal as a gender-equal nation.” It is identified the target indicators to fulfill in the year 2023/24 with reference to baseline data from 2018/19 tabulated below:

### 1.2 Gender and Energy Sector

Nationally, energy policies are crafted from a technical and gender-neutral perspective and have implications for equality between women and men and for the poor. Improvements in women's health, girls' education opportunities, household nutritional status, and livelihoods can be led by alternative energy technologies such as solar home systems (SHS), biogas digesters, and other small-scale technologies. However, maximizing these benefits requires attention to the barriers that women and excluded groups face accessing these technologies.

Major government policies in Nepal related to energy sector include the Electricity Act 1992, Hydropower Development Policy (2001), the National Water Resources Strategy (2002), 15th National Plan (2018-2023), the National Water Plan (2005), and the Rural Energy Policy (2006).

The legal and policy framework governing energy issues is largely silent on incorporating GGI as a factor, reflecting the lack of gender equality in these policies. For example, the Renewable Energy Subsidy Policy, 2018 is more relevant to the project components than other policies such as the Electricity Act, 1992.* For instance, the Rural Energy Policy, 2006, among others, explicitly states "as rural energy policy, it is important that all aspects of rural energy technology will be implemented considering it as an integral part of the women's enabling activities" (p. 3). This includes access to energy for productive uses, income generation, enhancing productivity of end users, and representation of excluded groups in user committees.

Policies which are more relevant to the project components would be Renewable Energy Subsidy Policy and Rural Energy Subsidy Delivery Mechanism, 2013 which have specific subsidies targeted to women and marginalized groups:

a. Additional subsidy of Rs. 5,000 per household will be provided to households with single woman, backward, disaster victim, conflict affected, poor and endangered ethnic group as identified by the government.

b. For solar thermal technologies for rural areas, additional Rs. 20,000 will be provided if at least 50 percent of the target groups are single women, backward, disaster victims, conflict victims, and poor households.

c. For biogas and metallic cookstoves, additional subsidies up to of NPR 4000 and 1000 are offered for single woman, backward, disaster victims, and endangered poor households, ethnic groups.


The National Rural Renewable Energy Programme (NRREP) managed by APEC as a 5-year programme that started in 2012, was an integrated version of different smaller programmes or projects. The


*UNDP (2019). Human Development Report 2019: Beyond Income, Beyond Equality, Beyond Friction. https://hdr.undp.org/sites/default/files/2019-05/hdr_2019_en.pdf

ADB. 2018 https://www.adb.org/sites/default/files/publication/76347/gender-equality-social-inclusion-assessment-energy- sector-nepal.pdf
