---
**Page 160**
---

NREGA programme mandated that specific affirmative actions were to be planned, implemented and monitored across all component activities, aiming to empower women and marginalized groups through enhancement of their technical capabilities and assisting them to take up ownership of technologies. A number of development programmes in Renewable Energy Sector of Nepal, such as the Bagmati Micro Hydro Power Project, Green Energy Nepal (GEN) and the ADB-supported Decentralized Rural Infrastructure and Livelihood Project (DRILP) provide good practices for GEN’s mission that can be learned and integrated into programme planning.

The GEN project has been implemented in a cross-sectoral manner by all levels of government of Government institutions and NGOs. For instance, FEDCONA, a Federation of Community Forest User Groups in Nepal, is one such example where the community forest user groups are part of the Steering Committee including people from all regions, indigenous peoples, minority groups, differently abled people, and ultra-poor. They practice Gender Equity and Social Inclusion (GESI) from federal to local level. FEDCONA has been able to mobilize women in their communities through a strong and vast network of grassroots women willing to contribute to women’s economic empowerment and ensuring that the benefits are shared equally by all. This is also working to leverage funds from local governments and co-finance the e-cooking sector.

In most of the programs, it is found that women have less access to information through official channels as reviewed by Roogman A., 2021. Also, the visibility of the programmes to involve women in technology development and use is low. The community forest user groups (CFUGs) are a market which we have tried to mitigate by setting specific targets of women participation and influencing their access to information. CFUGs have been able to create an effective network and most effective medium of communication and awareness in communities, learnt from their experiences in creating awareness in COVID pandemic or post-earthquake scenario.

An instrumental partner in the awareness creation and local marketing activities of the project could be the Community Health Volunteers (CHVs) who are trained by FEDCONA. It is reported that the community health interventions which are mobilised through FCHV have high coverage population and they can be an important channel for creating awareness on e-cooking technology, as well as promoting and influencing people to adopt the technology in their homes. CHVs were also trained on preventing COVID-19 transmission and pollution and further mobilized to train households on the benefits of clean-cooking solutions.

Leadership ability of women and girls in the energy sector are undermined by prevalent gender norms and discriminatory gender roles. There is no such consideration of gender issues and inclusive financing for women entrepreneurs, especially those who are from marginalized communities or those who want to initiate self-employed or micro enterprises. The barriers exist not just in the policy and legal framework but also in terms of access to information, programmes and implementation of energy and water sectors15. Moreover, there is no data available on aggregated data as a GIS in credit financing to market chain actors in IT technology.

### 2.1 Gender and Finances

The supply side of the energy sector has very few involvement of women technicians and experts. In the case of micro-hydro power households, they are more vulnerable than male heads to such external changes in terms of acquiring new knowledge and access to new resource bases16. Therefore, special


---
**Page 161**
---

measures have to be taken to monitor that women family members and female heads of households receive adequate assistance.

Gender stereotypes for women to have less education, early marriage, and total responsibility in household has hindered women to work as technical human resource. Unlike in cottage and small scale industries, interventions to promote women's involvement as technicians are insufficient. In the value chain of the livestock sector, women are major barrier in policy sectors while their active participation has been the same at local level.

There is no such consideration of gender issues and inclusive financing as well. There is priority loan provision but it is more focused on youth and poor people, who want to initiate self-employed or micro enterprises. The government has not taken any initiative for women's empowerment in the institutions, programmes and implementation of energy and water sectors[1]. No data is available on segregated loans for women. However, there are some initiatives that have been taken by few women led entrepreneurs in the clean cooking business that have been promoted by CRTN and other agencies. The Women Entrepreneurs Network (WENET) and Women's Energy Network (WEN) are two such examples. Similarly, Women's Network of Energy and Environment (WINNE) is a national network of grassroots women willing to contribute to women's economic empowerment and ensuring energy and environmental justice in Nepal[2]. WINNE has been working with CRTN for the promotion of clean cooking sector. In all provinces, there is no such initiative for GESI integration in the cookstove sector.

However, Nepal Rastra Bank directives to the banks and financial institution regulators then lead to lend up to 5% of their loan portfolio to the deprived sector that covers, low income, socially backward women, janai, dalit, and differently-abled persons[3]. Loan extended by Bifs to Mfs on corporate guarantee is also a good example. Similarly, credit financing through cooperative societies and cooperatives being member based organizations usually are successful in targeting GESI objectives which is a win-win situation for MFIs and women.

Most cooperatives and Mfs have more than 50% female members. Women have loan repayments above 95%. Therefore, these organizations are providing credit targeted beneficiaries for cookstoves. This approach has been a success also with win-win situations for BFIs, MFIs and cooperatives and strongly recommended for future acceleration of clean cooking projects[4].

Credit financing through multipurpose cooperatives have been in practice to support several types of small businesses. Few examples of same to purchase electric cookstoves in Kavre district have been taken up by CRTN. The credit provided is on a corporate guarantee basis, which is necessary to bear Capital expenses of cleaner cookstoves. This will create a conducive environment to make market sustainable.

### 1.2 Gender and clean cook stove

More than half (52%) of the households in the country use mud-stoves for cooking purposes, followed by kerosene/stove (21 percent), open fire-place (22 percent), and smokeless stove (3 percent)[5]. The gender dimension imposes influence on the household activities specially kitchen,

[1] Centre for Rural Technology Nepal 2018
[2] http://www.critep.org.np/images/2019/30Report2019%20without%20title.pdf
[3] Landscape Analysis of the cookstove Sector of Nepal, 2021
[4] Central Bureau of Statistics. (2011). Nepal living standards survey 2010–11.


---
**Page 162**
---

with girls and women bearing the majority of the negative effects of fuel collection and transport, household air pollution, and the use of time-consuming and unsafe cooking technologies. Overharvesting of firewood and fodder is also considered one of the major issues of deforestation in Nepal[16].

The present unemployment rate in Nepal is 11.4 percent where the rate of female (13.1%) unemployment is higher in comparison to male (10.3%)[2].

The gender assessment shows Nepal as a country with gender equality issues. Cooking is almost entirely done by women, requiring on average about 6 hours a day for cooking using a traditional cooking stove[3].

Women in Nepal spends about 142 minutes to collect the firewood whereas men spends only about 50 minutes[3]. Likewise, the study shows the involvement of women in collecting agricultural residue and cow dung is 70.2% whereas involvement of men is only 29.8%, this shows that in collecting both firewood and other residues women are more involved than men.

Despite having plenty of engagement in cooking, women have not been able to make decisions in selection of cookstoves. They have less or no access to information through official channels. For female headed households or single women, if they are illiterate, there are many challenges for them to collect firewood and other residues[3]. The study shows that 50% of the women surveyed said that they are not even able to utilize the benefits they are eligible to. These are the major barriers in the cookstove from gender's perspective, which could be somewhat addressed by the GAP activities 1.1.2 on promotion of clean cooking technologies and 3.1.1 on promoting energy efficiency.

In Nepal, on average, there are 18.3 kgs (headloads of about 30 kg on average) of firewood collection per capita per year in Nepal. On average, a household spends 5.01 hours collecting one bharon of firewood[4].

WHO estimated global 1.8 million people die yearly from pneumonia from illness attributable to the household air pollution caused by the inefficient use of solid fuels and kerosene for cooking. A study on Household Air Pollution by ENPHO found that the average 24 hour mean PM2.5 concentration in Nepali kitchens is 2127 μg/m3 with Traditional Cooking Stoves (TCS), and with ICS it is 728 μg/m3[5].

# Gender Assessment

## 2.1 GCF rationale for Gender Assessment

The GCF’s commitment to gender equality centers on gender-responsive country programmes and initiatives that benefit everyone, women and men. This requires gender analysis to understand the social, economic and political factors underlying climate change-exacerbated gender inequalities, and the potential contributions of women and men to mitigating and adapting to climate change, and building resilience in communities. Gender mainstreaming is a strategy for achieving gender equality and reduce gender disparities in climate funding by mainstreaming gender in the project cycle. Finally, it means measuring the outcomes and impacts of project activities on women’s and men’s resources and well-being through gender-responsive monitoring and evaluation (M&E). Gender mainstreaming should shape all the entire project cycle, from project identification to M&E.

## 2.2 Gender assessment

The project benefits about one million households, comprising 4,800,000 people (average family size of 4.8 people per household) in Teraf region. 37% of direct beneficiaries are female. The direct beneficiaries include 15% of the population living below poverty line and 17% of the total population of Teraf region are considered as indirect beneficiaries of the project which of 51% of the beneficiaries are female. The indirect beneficiaries of the project accounts for about 30% of total population.

The TCS users are the most vulnerable community as they are among the poorest and socially excluded groups: 23.44 % of the population in the Teraf region survive under absolute poverty. Against this, 38 % of the population are disqualified to cross the threshold of human poverty. In addition, by 25th January (indigenous group) and 30th June (Dalit population of Teraf live under extreme poverty).

### 2.2.1. Status of Gender Equality

#### Social norms and Practices

Social norms adversely contain gender inequalities. Traditionally, it is widely practiced for women to be involved more in the household activities like cooking, cleaning, laundry, gardening, and child care. Referring to the statistics, women are behind men in all the indicators in economic empowerment. The social norms also state that women should not work outside home as they should only work inside homes. Women are expected and even forced to have early marriages, which hinders the women to have equal opportunity as men. The women of indigenous communities and those below poverty line will have these hindrances amplified. Working women and women with income have good access to the project activities and benefits.

## Major Indicators and Political Participations

Female literacy rate, employment in professional sectors, education, political representation and political participation are relatively low, while labour force participation, life expectancy and population growth rates are high. This is because of the fact that male population Provisions of women reservations in political space and public sectors are there, but despite that, women involvement is less visible due to established boundaries and gender norms set for women. Major changes have been made since 2015 with regard to reservation of seats at local level and reserved nomination of 2 out of 6 ward members for a progressive sign observed since 2016 local elections. At least 33% of reservation for females in provincial and national assemblies is also supported.

### Policy environment

Gradually, the policies have been made to mainstream gender equality and social inclusion in all the sectors. The adoption of GES policies upto Provincial level has opened up an environment to take them to local levels. In the current fiscal year, several local governments have drafted their policies for GES integration and many others are in the process. However, a big challenge is to implement these policies at different levels. There are also some initiatives taken by the Federal and national levels by Non-Government Organizations, and other agencies to speed up the process. Several gender assessment reports and reviews submitted to the ministries and authorities have been made to mainstream gender equality and social inclusion in their gender friendly. The GES policy of APEC envisions to have equal and equitable access opportunities, and benefits of renewable energy to both the female and male based on their social status, ethnicity, age, religion, location, etc., and to provide them with equal opportunities. The objectives are to empower women, less privileged people by creating employment opportunities, to increase participation and capacitate them and to help overcome traditional social barriers so that everyone has the user to have equitable access to Renewable Energy Technologies by providing suitable opportunities.

## Legal Framework

The legal provisions of action against Gender based violence are secured in the Constitution of Nepal 2015 and Mukhi Act 2017.

## Poverty & Income

The per capita income stood at US $ 1,004 (Rs 103,385) in the fiscal year 2018/19 and the main portion of income goes for sustaining livelihoods. In 2016, Nepal achieved a score of 0.148 according to the multidimensional poverty index (MPI), with around 22.3% population subjected to Multidimensional Poverty (MP). 11.6 in severe MP and 15 % below income poverty line.

## Participation of Women in Family Economy

Nepali girls and women spend more than boys and men, spending 25% to 50% more time on household tasks, economic and agricultural activities. Yet, due to the nature of women's work, which is often unpaid, on the one hand, and the flawed definition of economic activity, on the other hand, women's economic participation remains statistically invisible.

### Education, Literacy and Information

Gender gaps in the education sector continue to persist, where girls and women tend to have lower levels of education, especially in rural areas. The percentage of women participating in primary education is 70% compared with 85% for boys; while the percentage of women completing 12 grade rises from 20% to 42%. Nonetheless, equality can still be promoted in terms of access to education, especially in rural areas where studies have found that girls' education often remains a lower priority.

## Land Ownership

Land and house ownership by females is very low compared to that of male. Despite having incentives like Land registration file for women's work, it is not able to have instrumental

# Health

Reproductive health has largely been funded by the government. Each local government has a number of family planning service providers and mobile units that provide services at health posts with birthing centres to promote hospital-based deliveries. Travel and maternity allowances are provided to all the hospital/health posts based delivery cases. Further, the Ministry of Health has also implemented family planning programmes in all its subsidiaries.

## Gender Based Violence

A nationally representative sample of 12,862 women aged 15-49 in 11,040 surveyed households and 4,063 men aged 15-49 in half of the surveyed households were interviewed. Based on that, the 2016 NHVS (National Health and Family Welfare Survey) provides reliable estimates at the national level for urban/rural areas, 5 ecological zones, 5 development regions, and 7 provinces.

A relatively high rate of gender violence has been observed in Province 2, which is also a focal area of the project and requires some additional measures to help reduce the risk of gender violence. However, the project impacts will not instigate any gender based violence.

## Gender and Energy(Environment)

Energy and Environment sections have been reorganized as gender neutral sectors, based on their technical nature. The National Rural Energy Policy 2006 has not been gender friendly. As alternative energy is a newer concept and institutionalized with the help of international grants,


---
**Page 167**
---

this has been most lenient to gender issues. Stakeholder consultation and public hearing programmes under Environmental Protection Acts seek for inclusive participation. Since the sector is of global concern, it is ahead of other sectors in mainstreaming Gender issues.

### Conclusion:
Barriers to an equal representation up and down in value chains includes traditional social norms biased against women, poverty as a result of limited access to education, early marriage, limited asset ownership. Furthermore, women’s participation is limited to decision making and income generating activities, and technical workforce.

## Gender Assessment Team
A core gender assessment team was formed, comprising:
- Key experts on clean cooking solutions
- GESI expert
- Government authorities and,
- Representatives of civil societies, including individual from the network of women right movement

In addition to the core gender assessment team, stakeholder consultation space was formed to incorporate inputs from energy and clean cooking experts, government officials, GESI experts, women representatives, and other stakeholders. The team included both national and local NGOs, private sectors (including the ones headed by women) in both service supply and service demand chain.

## Recommendations from stakeholder consultations [Annex 1]
- **Training and Awareness** should target the female (users) and not the household head. Training should be provided to women on electrical safety and basic technical skills on using induction cookers, which will increase their confidence.
- **Capacitate females to repair and maintain stoves to ensure sustainability and empower women**
- The poorest and most vulnerable communities cannot purchase the Tier 3+ ICs and even if the stoves are delivered free of cost, fuelwood is another major challenge. Most of them use one cooking stove at a time, which means they have limited access to available technologies. Research should be more focused on how male technologies compete with female owned fuel sources so the most vulnerable can assess the technologies.
- **Women and girls should be involved in decision-making** to effectively implement the project and the project should be targeted to the female and most vulnerable communities.
- Gender-specific targets should be set to ensure that certain numbers of female and male entrepreneurs are trained and employed to reflect the needs of women and men in their products, business models, and livelihood opportunities.

## Project Identified Opportunities to Challenge Gender Stereotypes

Some of the actions that are included in the project activities for the positive gender relations at project as follows:

1. The economically impoverished and socially excluded families, indigenous people, single women, female-headed households, and widows will be priorities while selecting beneficiary households.
2. The project will assist GCS in placing dedicated personnel related to environment safeguard and gender in GCS projects. In addition, they will be supported in conducting gender sensitive monitoring, evaluation and provide training on CCS and its benefits.
3. For the national level consultation workshop between AEPC, LGIs beneficiaries, clean cooking sector stakeholders and other relevant stakeholders, a set of guidelines for the project will be developed by drafting mechanisms to effectively address gender equality and supporting vulnerable communities through GCS.
4. The beneficiaries will receive technical assistance and capacity development services that will help to reduce their vulnerability and build resilience to climate change impact through the time saving, health benefits, gender empowerment and increase in agricultural productivity and income generation activities.
5. The project enhances women’s access and entitlement to benefits. Since women are direct users of the cook stoves, it will benefit women by reducing their exposure to indoor air pollution and improving their health status. In addition, the installation of more efficient stoves will reduce the workload of women to collect firewood, resulting in time saving that they can use for other productive activities such as getting an education or income generating activities. Therefore, 50% of the beneficiaries are the proposed CCS users.
6. The project will remove existing barriers in promotion of GCS to poorest households by preparing a manual for vulnerable communities and providing support and training on repair and maintenance of proposed technologies to women and marginalized groups to ensure inclusion and sustainability of the project. There are several local NGOs working in the clean cooking sector, which can be mobilized to resource their missions in awareness campaigns and skill development training.
7. A total of 24 Province level trainings will be provided to CCS beneficiaries and vulnerable groups in collaboration with CTEV Institutions. In addition, 1,200 training will be provided on manual for repair and maintenance of proposed technologies to women and marginalized groups (DAG). Training will be provided to women (electric cook stove users) on stove use and maintenance, which will increase their confidence and the use of the stove. Self-employed skilled trained related to CCS and others as per the skill demand will be provided to the most vulnerable people such as widows, single mothers, indigenous people, Dalits and other marginalized groups (Dalit) and indigenous group (Janjati), ensuring at least 33% female participation.
8. The project provides financial support for the proposed CCS technologies which will allow vulnerable user communities to transition to better CCS technologies with high returns.

# 3 Gender Action Plan (GAP)

GAP forms the basis for operationalising the results and recommendation of the gender assessment. It incorporates gender elements during project formulation (and design), project implementation and project monitoring & evaluation. GAP ensures necessary intention to mainstream gender and integrate gender perspective in the project to maximize clean cooking benefits. The GAP is compliant with GCF's gender policy and AEPC's Gender Equality & Social Inclusion policy.

The GAP is aligned with the outputs of the log frame and planned output and activities. Additionally, the programme will document the positive and negative effects the program activity will have on gender relations by setting up adequate, gender-sensitive monitoring and collecting sex-disaggregated data. Gender-specific language is used in reports, training materials and publications. Different partner entities and stakeholders (AEPC, LG, and vendors) will execute GAP in their program management capacity.

# Annex I: Stakeholder consultation Report

## Sub-national Stakeholder Consultation Meeting for "Mitigating GHG emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean cooking solutions (CCS)"

AEPC had called for an interaction program on 22nd June 2020 with National Stakeholder and relevant stakeholders on the proposed project Mitigating GHG emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean cooking solutions. The meeting was held virtually via Zoom. In total, around sixty people attended the meeting. It was facilitated by Mr Mukesh Ghimire, Assistant Director of AEPC, while Mr Rana Bahadur Shrestha, Deputy Executive Director of AEPC, provided necessary information from the participants. Mr Nawa Raj Dhakal, the Deputy Executive Director of AEPC gave the concluding remarks. The meetings enabled interested and concerned parties to contribute their concerns, suggestions and ideas on the project. The meeting was appreciated by all the participants. The participants appreciated the project and wished for its smooth implementation. Additionally, meaningful consultation will be conducted periodically during the implementation phase with the concerned stakeholders with active participation of women's, Dalits and vulnerable groups.

The tentative topics for discussion includes but not limited to:

1. Role of LGIs and involvement of NGOs at local level
   - Awareness generation among LGIs
   - Energy planning and Role of Local government in CCS promotion including funding (Modeling, Energy Plan, Implementation, Any CCS program ongoing or implemented, what role LGI can play for the success of CCS projects)
   - Any new policies/plans for CCS?
   - Roles currently working on CCS at local level and possibilities to engage them for the project

2. ESS Risks/Issues and mitigation measures
   - Key social, environmental, gender, economic and culture risks and issues with respect to CCS
   - Possible mitigation measures

3. Local level supply Chain
   - Baseline fuels for cooking
   - Capacity building of supply chain in meeting local demand.
   - Any information of supply and demand of CCS at local level?
   - Expectations to improve the supply chain

## Feedback
- It is very good idea to involve Provincial Energy Coordination Committee (PECC) in province 2, Province 5 and Karnali Province for prioritizing the demand as this committee includes representative from provincial ministries, provincial planning commission, NEA province office, etc.
- Training and Awareness should target the female (users) and not the household head.


---
**Page 180**
---

* Experience from the past should be analyzed to ensure financial, utility, commodity sustainability of the project
* Value chain of CCS (Supply to target group users) should be analyzed to fulfill the gap
* Move forward by catching up better experience and golden experienced
* Community Forests should be involved in the process
* Local governments should be consulted to reach the poorest of the poor
* We need to assure the quality of cookstoves and construction of biogas.
* Awareness campaign should focus on both male and female. The awareness should include rural women and communities who don't have NIA meters or the households who cannot keep the meter due to lack of land holding documents. Thus, we need to address these households and communities
* People in remote areas are not aware about CCS and its benefits. There is a lot of barriers. Biogas seems to have much demand compared to ICS as the numbers should increase.
* Community forest spends 25% of their total income in forest conservation and they have been involved in the project. Therefore, the community forest and vulnerable community can be integrated in community contribution for the project
* Previous World Bank funded, REED and other projects are more focused in the Terai region no major focus on CCS in the hills.
* Biogas and electric cooking does not benefit the poorest of the poor. Therefore, we need to explore more on how to make these pro-poor.
* Actual promotion should be done by creating demand and exploring the supply side
* Options of various technologies should be given to the user to ensure the use of the stoves after deployment
* Coordination with local government, involvement of females in stove making, distribution, and financial contribution of users creates the enabling environment for stove promotion. At present, each local government has female representatives. They should be enrolled as facilitators for promoting CCS.
* The poorest and most vulnerable communities cannot purchase the Tier 3+ ICS and even if the stoves are distributed free of cost, fuelwood is another major challenge. Most of them use one or two firewood per day. Therefore, promotion of biogas in these areas should be more focused to make technologies compatible with available local fuel sources so that the most vulnerable can assess the technologies. Research development dissemination and demonstration should be done.
* Cooperation, collaboration, and coordination should be done with the province level agriculture and forestry ministry
* Activities like training, awareness, and forward linkage of the project should be carried out parallel along with technology installation. For example, installation of biogas should be linked with promotion of bestowed farms and slurry management to promote the CCS.
* Stronger collaboration between the government and private sector is required for better service
* Stove design should meet the users' needs such as space heating, agro processing and commercial cooking.
* Access to information to the information (how are we giving information to the illiterate), low level of mobility of female should be included

* Coordination, collaboration, and coordination should be strong enough. LG has allocated much of its budget to renewable energy and women empowerment. Thus while identifying partners, criteria should be transparent to gain more weightage.
* Free distribution kills the market. Project should not have contradicting policies. In addition, total cost of ownership should be significantly eliminated.
* Monitoring has to be the part of implementation.
* Coordination with Province level ministries (Agricultural, Tourism, Forest and Environment) is necessary for successful implementation.
* Conservation significant area such as buffer zone, vicinity of forest area should be prioritized
* ICS favorable for water heating and space heating to be focused in high altitude area
* More focus on capacity building, training and empowerment and should be given to users directly at local level and less in trainings and awareness
* Extended to new geographical area to include Kaski parishad.
* NGOs should play a significant role in project implementation. Leadership and awareness campaigns and demand creation. Collaborate with NGO to effectively implement the project.
* Women promoters to effectively implement the project and target to the female and most vulnerable communities.
* Service centre at each province, repair maintenance centre, development of local association for sustainability, increase the project sustainability
* Collaborate with private sector, microfinance, NGOs,
* Projects should be demand driven and should be focused on need.
* Training and awareness campaigns to improve technical skills using induction stoves increases the use of stove and increase their confidence
* Capacitate female to repair and maintenance to ensure sustainability and empower women
* Research and development to make technologies more efficient rather to replicate the same old technologies
* Strengthen existing Network rather than establishing new ones and allocate more budget to users.
* Sustainability, after sales service and focus on user sensitive programs to increase the use
* Different financial package of support to poorest of the poor people to effectively implement the project.
* Shifting to electric cooking may take long time, technical efficiency to user and private sector
* Focused on Socially adaptation of technologies
* Technological barrier in adopting electric cookstoves sustainability
* Provincial/local government should share the cost in technology
* AEPC budget should be focused on enabling environment including the development of
* Need to consider the technological barrier in adopting the electric cookstoves.

# Green Climate Fund

## Financial Mechanism under UNFCCC
- **Objective**: To provide financial resources for developing countries to implement climate change mitigation and adaptation measures.
- **Key Components**:
    - **UNFCCC Secretariat**: Manages the fund.
    - **Trust Fund**: Provides initial funding.
    - **Investment Committee**: Decides on investments.

## Structure
- **Three Sectors**:
    1. **Climate Change Mitigation**
    2. **Climate Change Adaptation**
    3. **Technology Transfer**
